=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Takahashi, a patissier working at the ABC pastry shop, decided to sell assorted cakes to commemorate AtCoder Beginner Contest 400.
The shop sells N kinds of cakes: cake 1, cake 2, \ldots, cake N.
Each cake has three non-negative integer values: beauty, tastiness, and popularity. Specifically, cake i has beauty X_i, tastiness Y_i, and popularity Z_i.
He considers pairing up these cakes into K pairs without overlaps.
Formally, he will choose 2K distinct integers a_1,b_1,a_2,b_2,\ldots,a_K,b_K between 1 and N (inclusive), and pair cake a_i with cake b_i.
The price of a pair formed by cakes a_i and b_i is \max(X_{a_i} + X_{b_i},\, Y_{a_i} + Y_{b_i},\, Z_{a_i} + Z_{b_i}).
Here, \max(P,Q,R) denotes the greatest value among P,Q,R.
Find the maximum possible total price of the K pairs.
You are given T test cases; solve each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

\mathrm{case}_i represents the i-th test case. Each test case is given in the following format:
N K
X_1 Y_1 Z_1
X_2 Y_2 Z_2
\vdots
X_N Y_N Z_N

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line (1\leq i\leq T) should contain the answer to the i-th test case.

Constraints


- 1\leq T\leq 1000
- 2\leq N \leq 10^5
- The sum of N over all test cases in each input file is at most 10^5.
- 1\leq K \leq \lfloor \frac{N}{2}\rfloor (For a real number x, \lfloor x\rfloor denotes the greatest integer not exceeding x.)
- 0\leq X_i,Y_i,Z_i \leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

1
3 1
6 3 8
3 5 0
2 7 3

Sample Output 1

12

We form one pair out of three cakes.
If we pair cake 1 with cake 2, the price is \max(6+3,\,3+5,\,8+0) = 9.
If we pair cake 1 with cake 3, the price is \max(6+2,\,3+7,\,8+3) = 11.
If we pair cake 2 with cake 3, the price is \max(3+2,\,5+7,\,0+3) = 12.
Hence, pairing cake 2 with cake 3 gives the highest price, which is 12.

Sample Input 2

2
5 2
1 2 3
1 2 3
1 2 3
1 2 3
100 100 200
6 2
21 74 25
44 71 80
46 28 96
1 74 24
81 83 16
55 31 1

Sample Output 2

209
333

Note that each cake can appear in at most one pair.
Also note that there can be different cakes with identical values of beauty, tastiness, and popularity.
For the first test case, pairing cake 1 with cake 2 gives a price of 6, pairing cake 3 with cake 5 gives a price of 203, and choosing these two pairs yields a total price of 209, which is the maximum.  
For the second test case, pairing cake 2 with cake 3 gives a price of 176, pairing cake 4 with cake 5 gives a price of 157, and choosing these two pairs yields a total price of 333, which is the maximum.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

This is a key insight. Then we can solve the problem as a standard min-cost max-flow on the network:
S -> item i (cap1, cost0)
item i -> color c (cap1, cost -p_i^c)
color c -> T:
  - arc A: cap = 2K (or N? even), cost 0
  - arc B: cap = 1, cost M (large)
Need send exactly 2K units from S to T. The min-cost flow will send 2K units. Because M huge, it will avoid arc B; thus each color flow is even. The total cost = - total profit. Then answer = -cost.

Let's develop this properly.

We need solve the min-cost flow with parity constraints on color arcs. We can use the following algorithm:
1. Solve the unconstrained min-cost flow of value 2K in the network S->items->colors->T (with color->T capacity 2K). This gives a min-cost flow f (max profit) ignoring parity. Let cost C0.
2. If all color flows f_c are even, answer = -C0.
3. Else, exactly two colors have odd flow. Let odd set O = {a,b}. We need find a minimum cost circulation δ in the residual graph of f such that the parity of the change on color arcs is O (i.e., toggles a and b). Then f+δ is a feasible flow with even color flows. Its cost is C0 + cost(δ). Since f is unconstrained optimal, any circulation with zero parity has nonnegative cost; the minimum cost circulation with parity O gives the optimal even flow? Need prove: For any even flow f', f'-f is a circulation with parity O (because f'_c even, f_c odd for c∈O, so change odd; for even colors change even). Thus cost(f') = C0 + cost(f'-f). The minimum cost even flow equals C0 + minimum cost circulation in the residual graph with parity O. This is true if the residual graph is with respect to f and capacities allow f'-f. Since f is optimal for the unconstrained problem, the residual graph has no negative cycles (by min-cost flow optimality). The minimum cost circulation with parity O can be found. Good.

Now, how to find the minimum cost circulation with parity O in this specific residual graph efficiently?

The residual graph R has nodes: S, T, items, colors. Edges:
- S->i for unused items (cost 0, cap1)
- i->S for used items (cost 0, cap1) (reverse of S->i)
- i->c for unused item-color edges (cost -p_i^c, cap1)
- c->i for used item-color edges (cost p_i^c, cap1)
- c->T for color arcs with remaining capacity (cost 0, cap 2K - f_c)
- T->c for color arcs with flow f_c (cost 0, cap f_c)

We need a circulation δ in R. Since R is a directed graph, a circulation can be decomposed into directed cycles. The parity condition is on the change of original color arcs. Let's define for each color c, the original arc a_c = (c -> T). The change Δ_c = δ(a_c) - δ(T->c). We need Δ_c odd for c∈O, even otherwise. Since δ is a circulation, the parity of Δ_c equals δ(a_c) + δ(T->c) mod2 (because - same). Let's call the two residual arcs between color c and T: forward F_c = (c->T), reverse R_c = (T->c). We need the total flow on {F_c, R_c} in δ to be odd for c∈O, even otherwise.

Now, consider the residual graph with the three color nodes and T. The only edges incident to T are F_c and R_c (plus maybe? T also has edges to items? No, T is sink, only color->T and T->color. S is source, but in residual, T has no other edges. S has edges to items and items to S. A circulation cannot include S or T? It can, but since S and T have no net supply, cycles can pass through them. However, any cycle that uses F_c or R_c must enter/leave T. Since T's only incident edges are these color arcs, a cycle that includes T must use exactly two of these arcs (one entering T, one leaving T) unless it uses more. The parity of total flow on F_c/R_c for a color c in a cycle is 0 if the cycle uses both F_c and R_c? Let's analyze.

A directed cycle in R that includes T must have one edge entering T and one edge leaving T (because T has no other edges, and a simple cycle visits T once). The edge entering T must be some F_c (color c -> T). The edge leaving T must be some R_d (T -> color d). Thus a cycle through T corresponds to a path from color d to color c in the subgraph without T, plus R_d and F_c. It toggles parity of color d (via R_d) and color c (via F_c). Wait, if d=c, it uses R_c and F_c, toggling color c twice? The change Δ_c = δ(F_c) - δ(R_c) = 1 - 1 = 0, so parity even. So a cycle T->c ... c->T toggles no color parity (net zero). If d≠c, it toggles both d and c (one reverse, one forward), so the parity vector has two 1s. Thus any simple cycle through T toggles either zero or two colors. A circulation with parity O={a,b} can be a single cycle through T that toggles a and b, or a combination of cycles. Since we want minimum cost, it will be a single shortest cycle/path? Let's see.

A cycle through T that toggles a and b consists of either:
- T -> a (reverse arc R_a) + path from a to b in R without T + F_b (b -> T). This decreases f_a by 1 and increases f_b by 1. Cost = 0 + dist(a,b) + 0 = dist(a,b).
- T -> b + path b to a + F_a, cost = dist(b,a).
It could also use T->a, path a to T? That would be a cycle T->a ... F_a, toggling a twice (net zero). Not useful.
It could use T->a, path a to b, F_b, then? That's already a cycle returning to T via F_b. Yes.

What about a cycle that doesn't pass through T but toggles color arcs? Color arcs are only incident to T, so any cycle using F_c or R_c must pass through T. Thus all parity-changing cycles pass through T. Therefore the minimum cost circulation with parity O is the minimum cost of a path from a to b or b to a in the residual graph R without T, plus the zero-cost arcs to T. Specifically:
cost = min( dist_R_without_T(a, b), dist_R_without_T(b, a) )
where dist from color node a to color node b in the residual graph excluding T. Because we can take T->a (cost0), path a->b, b->T (cost0). This circulation changes f_a by -1 (via reverse) and f_b by +1 (via forward), toggling a and b. If we take T->b + path b->a + a->T, it changes b -1 and a +1. Both toggle a,b. The cost is the shortest path between the two odd color nodes in the residual graph (excluding T). Is that sufficient? What if the minimum circulation uses a cycle that toggles a and b but also includes extra zero-parity cycles? Those would add nonnegative cost, so not needed. What if the shortest path from a to b uses an item that is also used in another part? A path is simple, so fine. Thus the minimum correction cost is the shortest path distance between the two odd colors in the residual graph.

  d[a][c]+d[b][c]
).
But note d[c][a]+d[c][b] and d[a][c]+d[b][c] are different; both are valid. Also what about d[a][c]+d[c][b]? That's a path a-c and c-b, endpoints a,b (c twice), cost d[a][c]+d[c][b]. This is a valid two-path option with common color c but paths are a->c and c->b. Endpoints: a,c,c,b -> a,b. Yes! So any two paths that form a walk from a to b via c? Actually d[a][c]+d[c][b] is a path a->c plus path c->b, which together form a walk from a to b with a cycle? It toggles a and b (c twice). This is also valid. More generally, any combination of paths whose endpoint parity is O. Since paths are directed, the parity only depends on endpoints, not orientation. So we can choose any two paths whose endpoints multiset has odd a,b. The possible endpoint multisets:
- {a,b}: one path between a and b (either direction).
- {a,c} and {b,c}: two paths sharing c. The orientations of each path can be either direction, so costs can be d[a][c] or d[c][a], and d[b][c] or d[c][b]. Thus four combinations.
- {a,b} and {c,c}? A path from c to c toggles none, not useful.
- {a,b} and {a,b}? two paths both between a,b toggles a,b twice -> zero, not useful.
So correction = min of:
d[a][b], d[b][a],
d[a][c]+d[b][c], d[a][c]+d[c][b], d[c][a]+d[b][c], d[c][a]+d[c][b].

Wait a path from a to c in residual graph, to form a cycle through T, we need enter T from c? Let's define a "path" between colors u and v in the residual graph excluding T. To make a cycle through T that toggles u and v, we can use either:
- T -> u (reverse) + path u -> v + v -> T (forward): toggles u (decrease) and v (increase). Cost d[u][v].
- T -> v + path v -> u + u -> T: cost d[v][u].
So a single path from u to v toggles u and v regardless of direction? Actually if we use path u->v, the cycle is T->u ... v->T, toggles u,v. If we use path v->u, cycle T->v ... u->T, toggles u,v. So the cost for toggling pair {u,v} is min(d[u][v], d[v][u])? Wait, for a single pair, we can choose either orientation, so cost = min(d[u][v], d[v][u]). But in the two-path combination, each path toggles its endpoints. For example, paths a->c and b->c: To use path a->c as a cycle, we need T->a + path a->c + c->T, toggles a,c. To use path b->c, T->b + path b->c + c->T, toggles b,c. Sum toggles a,b. Cost d[a][c]+d[b][c]. Alternatively, path c->a uses T->c + path c->a + a->T, toggles c,a. So orientation matters: the cost of a path from u to v is d[u][v] and it toggles {u,v} (same as d[v][u] would also toggle {u,v} but with opposite direction of flow changes). Since we only care parity, both orientations are valid. Thus for any ordered pair (u,v), cost d[u][v] toggles {u,v}. So to achieve parity O, we can take any multiset of ordered paths whose endpoint pairs (as unordered) have odd degrees at a,b. The cost is sum of d[u][v] for each path. Therefore the minimum is the minimum weight T-join in a complete directed graph with edge weights d[u][v] (where d[u][u]=0? A path u->u toggles none, cost 0, not useful). For 3 vertices, the T-join for odd set {a,b} is either one edge {a,b} with cost min(d[a][b], d[b][a])? Wait if we take one ordered path a->b, cost d[a][b]. We could also take b->a cost d[b][a]. Since we can choose either, the min is min(d[a][b], d[b][a]). But in the two-path option, we can choose orientations independently. So correction = min(
  d[a][b], d[b][a],
  d[a][c]+d[b][c],
  d[a][c]+d[c][b],
  d[c][a]+d[b][c],
  d[c][a]+d[c][b]
).
But note d[a][c]+d[c][b] is a path a->c and c->b. This toggles {a,c} and {c,b} -> a,b. Valid. It might be cheaper than d[a][b] if going through c is cheaper. This is just the shortest path distance from a to b in the color graph with edge weights d? Actually d[a][c]+d[c][b] is a walk from a to b via c, but since d already is shortest path in residual graph, d[a][b] ≤ d[a][c]+d[c][b]? Not necessarily because d is not metric (directed, no triangle inequality? It should satisfy triangle inequality: d[u][v] ≤ d[u][w]+d[w][v] because concatenating paths gives a path from u to v. Yes, shortest path distances satisfy triangle inequality. So d[a][c]+d[c][b] ≥ d[a][b]. Similarly d[c][a]+d[c][b] is not a walk from a to b; it's two paths from c to a and c to b. This can be cheaper than d[a][b]. So the relevant two-path options are those sharing a common start or end? Let's systematically enumerate T-joins on 3 vertices with odd set {a,b}. The possible edge sets (multisets) in an undirected graph with odd degrees a,b:
- Single edge {a,b}: cost min(d[a][b], d[b][a])? Actually if we use one directed path a->b, cost d[a][b]; or b->a, cost d[b][a]. We can choose the cheaper orientation, so cost min(d[a][b], d[b][a]).
- Two edges {a,c} and {b,c}: In an undirected T-join, the cost would be w(a,c)+w(b,c) where w(u,v)=min(d[u][v], d[v][u])? But because paths are directed, we can orient each edge independently. The cost is min over orientations of d[orientation1]+d[orientation2]. For edge {a,c}, orientation can be a->c or c->a; for {b,c}, b->c or c->b. So cost = min(
  d[a][c]+d[b][c],
  d[a][c]+d[c][b],
  d[c][a]+d[b][c],
  d[c][a]+d[c][b]
).
This is exactly the minimum of four combinations. This is the cost of a "star" centered at c with two paths, each can be directed either way.
- Three edges forming a triangle has all degrees even (2), so not useful.
Thus correction = min(single_edge_cost, star_cost).

Now, in the counterexample, odd set {Y,Z}, even color X.
d[Y][Z]=50, d[Z][Y]=50 -> single=50.
Star centered X:
d[X][Y]=1, d[X][Z]=1 -> cost 2. Also d[Y][X]+d[Z][X]=100, d[X][Y]+d[Z][X]=51, d[Y][X]+d[X][Z]=51. Min=2. Correction=2. Great! Then even optimal cost = C0 + 2 = -198, profit 198. Perfect.

So the algorithm is:
1. Solve unconstrained min-cost flow of value 2K in the network S -> items -> colors -> T with color->T capacity 2K (or N? Need capacity at least 2K; use 2K). This gives optimal f and cost C0.
2. Compute the residual graph shortest path distances d[u][v] between the three color nodes (u,v ∈ {0,1,2}) in the residual graph excluding T (and excluding S? Paths between colors go through items; they don't need S/T. They may use S? A path from color u to color v could go color u -> item -> S -> item -> color v? Let's see residual edges: color -> item (reverse of item->color) for assigned items; item -> S (reverse of S->item) for used items; S -> item for unused items; item -> color for unassigned item-color edges. Could a shortest path between colors use S? Yes, it could go color u -> item i (assigned to u) -> S (if i used) -> item j (unused) -> color v. This would correspond to a circulation that also changes which items are selected? Wait, a circulation in the residual graph can include S and T. Paths between colors in the residual graph excluding T may pass through S. Is that allowed? Let's examine.

The residual graph includes S and T. A cycle through T that toggles colors uses T->a, path a->b, b->T. The path a->b in the residual graph could pass through S. Is that a valid circulation? Yes, because S has flow conservation in the circulation (net zero). But what does it mean operationally? It might involve selecting an unused item and unselecting a used item, effectively swapping selected status. This is allowed in a circulation because the total flow value remains 2K. So we must include S in the graph for shortest paths. However, S is connected to items and items to S. We need compute shortest paths between color nodes in the full residual graph excluding T (but including S). The residual graph also has edges from items to S (reverse of S->item) and S->items. These can create paths.

Do we need to include T? We exclude T because paths between colors should not use T; if they used T, that would be a cycle with additional color toggles. The shortest path between colors in the residual graph without T is what we need. We can include S.

But wait, the residual graph also has edges from T to colors and colors to T. We exclude T to avoid using color arcs. We can compute distances from each color node to each other color node in the graph R' = residual graph with T removed. R' has nodes: S, items, colors. Edges:
- S -> i for unused items (cost 0)
- i -> S for used items (cost 0)
- i -> c for item i not assigned to c (cost -p_i^c)
- c -> i for item i assigned to c (cost p_i^c)
No color->T or T->color.
We need d[u][v] for u,v in colors in R'. This is a graph with N items + S + 3 colors. We can compute shortest paths from each color to all other colors? Only 3 sources, but N up to 1e5. Need efficient.

The graph R' has a special structure: It is a bipartite-like graph between items and colors, plus S connected to items both directions. Edges:
- S -> i: if item unused (not assigned to any color). Cost 0.
- i -> S: if item used (assigned to some color). Cost 0.
- i -> c: if item i is not assigned to color c. Cost -p_i^c.
- c -> i: if item i is assigned to color c. Cost p_i^c.
Note that if item i is assigned to color a, then:
  - c -> i exists only for c=a with cost p_i^a.
  - i -> c exists for c≠a with cost -p_i^c.
  - i -> S exists.
  - S -> i does NOT exist (since item used).
If item i is unused:
  - S -> i exists.
  - i -> S does NOT exist.
  - i -> c exists for all c with cost -p_i^c.
  - c -> i does NOT exist for any c.

We need shortest paths between color nodes. Since there are only 3 colors, we can compute distances by considering paths that alternate color -> item -> color, possibly with S in between. A path from color u to color v can be:
- Direct: u -> item i (assigned to u) -> v (if i not assigned to v, i.e., v≠u). Cost p_i^u - p_i^v. This is the reassignment cost of moving item i from u to v.
- Via S: u -> item i (assigned to u) -> S -> item j (unused) -> v. Cost p_i^u + 0 + 0 - p_j^v = p_i^u - p_j^v. This corresponds to unselecting item i and selecting unused item j for color v, while color u loses an item? Actually the cycle would also involve T? Let's check if this path is valid in a circulation. A path u->...->v in R' is used in a cycle T->u + path + v->T. The path u->i->S->j->v means: start at color u, take reverse edge u->i (unassign i from u), then i->S (unselect i), then S->j (select unused j), then j->v (assign j to v). Net effect: item i becomes unselected, item j becomes selected and assigned to v. This changes total selected items? In the cycle, T->u decreases f_u by 1, v->T increases f_v by 1. The path also unselects i and selects j, so total selected items remains 2K. This is a valid alternating path. Good.

- Paths can be longer: u -> i1 -> v -> i2 -> u? But shortest between colors can be computed by considering all possible transitions. Since only 3 colors, the shortest path from u to v might go through other colors: u -> i1 -> w -> i2 -> v. This is a sequence of reassignments. We need compute all-pairs shortest paths among the 3 colors in R'. Because the graph has only 3 color nodes, we can compute the shortest path distances using a variant of the Floyd-Warshall if we know the cost of direct transitions between colors, including via S.

Let's define for each ordered pair of colors (a,b), the cost of a single "step" from color a to color b in R':
- If a == b, cost 0 (stay).
- If a != b, the cheapest way to go from color a to color b without passing through another color (i.e., via one item) is:
   min over items i assigned to a of (p_i^a - p_i^b)  (using i->b)
   and also via S: min over i assigned to a, j unused of (p_i^a - p_j^b) = min_assigned_a p_i^a + min_unused (-p_j^b)? Actually p_i^a - p_j^b. To minimize, choose i with min p_i^a among assigned to a, and j with max p_j^b among unused (since -p_j^b). But this path goes a->i->S->j->b. It does not pass through another color. It is a valid direct transition from a to b in R'. There may also be a->i->S->j->c->... longer.
- Also a->i->b is a direct transition. The shortest path from a to b could use a sequence of colors: a -> c -> b, where each color transition uses one item. Since there are only 3 colors, we can compute the shortest path in a complete directed graph on 3 colors where the edge weight w[a][b] is the minimum cost of a single transition from color a to color b (a≠b) in R' that does not go through another color? But a transition a->b via an item assigned to a is exactly one edge. A transition via S is also one edge. Longer paths are captured by shortest paths in the color graph with these edge weights. However, we must be careful: The cost of a transition from a to b using an item assigned to a and then assigning it to b changes the assignment: the item moves from a to b. If a path later uses a transition from b to c, it needs an item assigned to b. But after the first transition, the item is assigned to b, so it could be used again for the next transition? In the residual path, can the same item be used twice? A simple path cannot repeat vertices, but the compressed color graph might allow using the same item in consecutive transitions if we are not careful. Earlier we argued an item assigned to a is not assigned to b before augmentation, so it cannot be used for a transition from b to c. But after the first transition in the path, the item is conceptually moved to b, but the residual path hasn't augmented yet; the residual graph still has the item assigned to a. The edge b->c in the residual graph requires an item currently assigned to b. The item just used is not currently assigned to b, so it cannot be used. Thus the color graph edge weights should be based on the current assignment before augmentation, and a path a->b->c uses two distinct items: one assigned to a for a->b, and one assigned to b for b->c. This is valid. If we compute edge weights w[a][b] = min over items assigned to a of (p_i^a - p_i^b), and w[b][c] = min over items assigned to b of (p_j^b - p_j^c), they use items from different color classes, so distinct. Good.

What about the via S transition a->i->S->j->b: it uses an item i assigned to a and an unused item j. If the path later goes b->c, it needs an item assigned to b. The unused item j is now assigned to b in the path, but before augmentation it is unused, so it cannot be used for b->c. Thus edge weights using current assignment are valid. However, if we use the same unused item j in two different transitions? An unused item is not assigned to any color, so it can only appear in a via S transition as the second item. It cannot appear in a color->item reverse edge. So no conflict.

Therefore, we can compute a 3x3 matrix w[a][b] representing the minimum cost of a single "move" from color a to color b in the residual graph R' (a≠b), using either:
- a -> item assigned to a -> b: cost p_i^a - p_i^b.
- a -> item assigned to a -> S -> unused item -> b: cost p_i^a - p_j^b.
Are there other direct single moves? What about a -> item assigned to a -> S -> item used in another color? From S, residual edges only go to unused items (S->i) because used items have i->S, not S->i. So no.
What about starting from color a, we could go to an item assigned to a, then to S, then to an unused item, then to a color b, yes.
Could we go color a -> item assigned to a -> S -> unused item -> S? No, unused item has no i->S.
Could we go color a -> item assigned to a -> item? No item-item edges.
Thus any path in R' alternates colors and items, with possible S between an assigned item and an unused item. The shortest path between colors can be represented as a path in a graph with 3 color nodes plus S? Actually S is just a zero-cost intermediate. We can incorporate it into edge weights.

But wait, a path could go color a -> item i (assigned to a) -> color b -> item k (assigned to b) -> color c. This is captured by color graph edges a->b and b->c. Good.

A path could go color a -> item i (assigned to a) -> S -> unused item j -> color b -> item k (assigned to b) -> color c. This is captured by edge a->b via S, then b->c. Good.

A path could go color a -> item i (assigned to a) -> S -> unused item j -> color b -> S? From color b, to go to S, we need color b -> item assigned to b -> S. That's a transition b->? via S? Actually color b -> item k (assigned to b) -> S. This would be a path from b to S, not between colors. But in a path between colors, we don't need to go to S except as intermediate. The color graph with edges a->b as above should capture all shortest paths because any time we go from a color to S, we must have come from a color via an assigned item, and then from S to an unused item to a color. So S appears only in a transition from some color to another color. Thus we can define w[a][b] to include the possibility of going through S exactly once. Could a path go through S multiple times? Yes, e.g., a -> i -> S -> j -> b -> k -> S -> l -> c. This is captured by edge a->b (via S) then b->c (via S). So color graph with edges w[a][b] works.

Now, how to compute w[a][b] efficiently?
For a≠b:
w[a][b] = min(
  min_{i assigned to a} (p_i^a - p_i^b),
  min_{i assigned to a} p_i^a + min_{j unused} (-p_j^b)
)
= min(
  min_assigned_a (p_i^a - p_i^b),
  min_assigned_a p_i^a - max_unused p_j^b
)
But note the second term uses the same item i for the first part and an unused item j. It is valid. We can maintain:
- For each color a, the minimum over assigned items of (p_i^a - p_i^b) for each b.
- For each color a, the minimum p_i^a among assigned items.
- For each color b, the maximum p_j^b among unused items.

Then w[a][b] = min( min_diff[a][b], min_assigned_value[a] - max_unused_value[b] ).

But is the via S transition always a valid single move in the residual graph? Let's verify residual edges:
color a -> item i: reverse of item i -> color a, exists if i assigned to a, cost p_i^a.
item i -> S: reverse of S -> item i, exists if i used (assigned to some color), cost 0. Yes, i is used.
S -> item j: forward, exists if j unused, cost 0.
item j -> color b: forward, exists if j not assigned to b (j unused, so yes), cost -p_j^b.
So path a->i->S->j->b is valid. Good.

Now, after computing w[a][b] for all ordered pairs, we need the shortest path distances d[a][b] in the complete directed graph on 3 colors with edge weights w (and d[a][a]=0). Since there are only 3 nodes, we can run Floyd-Warshall or simply:
d = w copy (with 0 diagonal)
for k in 0..2:
  for i in 0..2:
    for j in 0..2:
      d[i][j] = min(d[i][j], d[i][k]+d[k][j])
But wait, w[a][b] as defined is the cost of a single transition from a to b using one item assigned to a (and possibly an unused item). A path a->b->c uses two transitions. The shortest path distances d will capture sequences of transitions. However, is it possible that the shortest path from a to b uses the same item in two different transitions? We argued no because an item assigned to a cannot be used for a transition from b to c. But what about a path a->b->a? It would use an item assigned to a for a->b, and an item assigned to b for b->a. Distinct. Good.

But there is a subtlety: The transition cost w[a][b] via S uses an unused item j. If the path later uses a transition from b to c via S, it might use the same unused item j? After the first transition, j is assigned to b in the augmented flow, but before augmentation it is unused. In the residual path, after reaching color b, to go to S again, we need color b -> item k assigned to b -> S. The unused item j is not assigned to b before augmentation, so it cannot be used for b->S. Thus no conflict. If the path uses w[b][c] via S, it will use a different unused item l. So distinct.

What about the via S transition w[a][b] uses min_assigned_a p_i^a and max_unused p_j^b. It doesn't require i and j to be distinct? i is assigned, j is unused, so distinct automatically. Good.

Now, after computing correction cost, we need to actually apply the correction? We only need the final answer, not the matching. The final answer = - (C0 + correction). Because C0 is min cost (negative profit) of unconstrained optimal. Correction is the minimum additional cost (positive) to fix parity. The final profit = -C0 - correction. We don't need to output the matching. So we don't need to update assignments. Great! We just need compute C0 and correction from the unconstrained optimal assignment.

But wait: Is the unconstrained optimal flow unique? The correction formula assumes we start from an unconstrained optimal flow f. If there are multiple unconstrained optimal flows, the minimum correction cost might depend on which f we choose. We need choose an f that minimizes C0 + correction. The standard min-cost flow algorithm returns some optimal f. The parity-corrected optimum is min_{even f'} cost(f') = min_{f unconstrained optimal} (C0 + min_circ_parity(f))? Actually any even flow f' is a feasible flow. Let f* be an unconstrained optimal flow. For any even flow f', f' - f* is a circulation with parity O(f*) and cost cost(f') - C0. Thus cost(f') = C0 + cost(circ). The minimum over f' is C0 + min cost circulation with parity O(f*) in the residual graph of f*. This is valid for any unconstrained optimal f*. Because f' - f* is always a circulation (same supply) and its parity is determined by f*'s odd colors. So the formula holds for any unconstrained optimal f*. The minimum cost circulation with parity O in the residual graph of f* gives the best even flow reachable from f*. Since every even flow is reachable from f* by some circulation, the minimum over all even flows equals C0 + min circulation. This does not depend on which f* we use, as long as C0 is the unconstrained optimum. Is that true? Suppose f*1 and f*2 are both unconstrained optimal. The set of even flows is fixed. For f*1, min circ gives min_{even f'} cost(f') - C0. For f*2, same. So yes, any unconstrained optimal works. Good.

Now, we need solve the unconstrained min-cost flow of value 2K in the network S->items->colors->T with color->T capacity 2K (or N? Since each color can take at most N items, but total flow 2K, capacity 2K is enough; if a color takes 2K items, that's all items selected, possible if N≥2K. Use capacity 2K). We can use the compressed SSP described earlier, but now without parity. We need compute the optimal assignment of 2K items to colors (each item at most one color) maximizing sum p_i^c, with no restriction on color counts (except capacity 2K). This is a min-cost flow with 3 colors. We can solve it using the same compressed SSP unit-by-unit, which is O(K log N) with small constants. Let's design that carefully.

Unconstrained problem:
Maximize ∑ p_i^c x_i^c
s.t. ∑_c x_i^c ≤1 for each item
∑_i x_i^c ≤ 2K (capacity; actually no need but use 2K)
∑_{i,c} x_i^c = 2K.

This is a maximum cost flow. We can solve by successive shortest augmenting path in the residual graph. Since only 3 colors, each augmentation assigns one item to a color, possibly with reassignments. We can use the same color graph w as above, but now the sink T has capacity remaining for colors. The shortest augmenting path from S to T in the residual graph (including T) can be represented as:
S -> unused item i -> color a -> (possibly transitions through colors) -> color b -> T.
Cost = min_{unused i} (-p_i^a) + path cost from a to b + 0 if color b has remaining capacity.
We can compute the shortest path from S to T in a graph with S, T, and 3 color nodes, where:
- S -> color a: cost s[a] = min_{unused item i} (-p_i^a) (assign an unused item to a).
- color a -> color b: w[a][b] as above (reassign one item from a to b, possibly via S).
- color b -> T: cost 0 if flow_b < capacity (2K), else ∞.

Then the shortest augmenting path cost is min over a,b of s[a] + d[a][b] + t[b], where d is shortest path distances in the color graph with edge weights w. Since only 3 colors, we can compute this easily.

After augmenting along the path, we need update the assignment:
- If path is S -> unused item i -> color a -> ... -> color b -> T, we assign i to a, and for each transition color u -> color v along the path, move one item from u to v (the item that achieved the minimum w[u][v]).
We need maintain data structures to query:
- s[a]: min over unused items of (-p_i^a), i.e., max p_i^a among unused.
- w[a][b]: min over items assigned to a of (p_i^a - p_i^b), and also via S term min_assigned_a p_i^a - max_unused_b p_j^b.
- t[b]: whether color b has remaining capacity (flow_b < 2K).

We also need to retrieve the actual item for each transition to update assignments. For w[a][b] via direct item, we need the item i assigned to a that minimizes p_i^a - p_i^b. For via S, we need an item i assigned to a with min p_i^a and an unused item j with max p_j^b. When augmenting, we need apply the path: assign unused item to first color; for each color transition u->v, move the chosen item from u to v. This changes the assignment of that item from u to v. It also changes the unused set (the starting unused item becomes used; if the path used via S, the unused item j becomes used, and the item i moved from a to b remains used; no item becomes unused? Wait, the path S->unused item i0 -> color a means i0 becomes used. The path may also include transitions via S: color u -> item i (assigned to u) -> S -> unused item j -> color v. This transition in an augmenting path from S to T: It unselects item i? Let's trace a full augmenting path:
S -> i0 (unused) -> color a (assign i0 to a)
Then transition a->b via direct: color a -> item i1 (assigned to a) -> color b. This moves i1 from a to b.
If transition a->b via S: color a -> item i1 (assigned to a) -> S -> item j (unused) -> color b. This means i1 becomes unselected? Let's see residual path: a->i1 (reverse of i1->a) unassigns i1 from a; i1->S (reverse of S->i1) unselects i1; S->j selects j; j->b assigns j to b. Net: i1 becomes unused, j becomes assigned to b. So the number of used items remains the same? We started with i0 becoming used, and j becoming used, i1 becoming unused. Net used count +1 (i0) -1 (i1) +1 (j) = +1. Good. So an item can become unused during an augmentation. This is important for data structures.

Thus augmenting paths can involve both unused and used items changing status. We need update:
- The starting unused item i0: unused -> assigned to a.
- For each transition u->v:
   - If direct: item i assigned to u moves from u to v (remains used).
   - If via S: item i assigned to u becomes unused; unused item j becomes assigned to v.
So the set of unused items changes: i0 removed, possibly some i removed, some j added.

We need maintain for each color:
- The multiset of items assigned to that color, with their p values.
- The multiset of unused items, with their p values for each color.

Since N up to 1e5 and augmentations up to 2K ≤1e5, we can use heaps or sorted sets. Python doesn't have a built-in balanced BST, but we can use heaps with lazy deletion. For each color, we need:
- min over assigned items of (p_i^a - p_i^b) for each b. This is a value depending on b. We can maintain for each color a and each b a heap of (p_i^a - p_i^b, item) for items currently assigned to a. When an item moves, we need remove it from the old color's heaps and add to new color's heaps. Lazy deletion: each item has a current color (or unused). For a heap for (a,b), when querying min, pop while the item at top is not currently assigned to a. This works because each item is in at most one color; we can push entries when item assigned to a, and ignore stale if current_color != a.
- min over assigned items of p_i^a for each color a. This is just the minimum p_i^a among assigned to a. We can maintain a heap of (p_i^a, item) for items assigned to a, lazy by current_color == a.
- max over unused items of p_i^b for each color b. Maintain a heap of (-p_i^b, item) for unused items, lazy by current_color == -1 (unused). Actually unused items are not assigned to any color; current_color = -1. We can push all items as unused initially? Initially no items assigned, all unused. We need max p_i^b among unused. Use max-heap via negative.

- s[a] = min over unused items of (-p_i^a) = - max p_i^a among unused. So we can use the same unused max-heap for each color: s[a] = -max_unused[a].

- t[b]: flow count per color (number of items assigned to b). We need capacity 2K. Initially 0. After augmentation, update flow counts for colors involved:
   - Starting color a: flow +1 (i0 assigned to a).
   - For each transition u->v: flow_u -1, flow_v +1 (because item moves from u to v; if via S, i1 becomes unused so u loses one, j gains one for v; net flow_u--, flow_v++).
   - If path ends at color b, flow_b +1? Let's check: The augmenting path from S to T increases total flow by 1. The color at the end (b) has flow +1 due to the final item entering T? In the residual path, the last transition into b and then b->T means the item assigned to b (either moved or newly assigned) exits to T? Actually flow on color->T increases by 1, so total flow through color b increases by 1. The internal transitions conserve flow at intermediate colors: each intermediate color loses one item to next and gains one from previous, net 0. The starting color a gains the new item from S, net +1. The ending color b loses one item to T? Wait, in the residual path, b->T is the forward color arc, increasing flow on b->T by 1. For flow conservation at color b, if the path enters b from previous transition, that item is assigned to b; then b->T sends one unit to T. The flow on b->T increases by 1, but the number of items assigned to b? Let's think in terms of original flow: An augmenting path in the residual network increases the flow on S->item, item->color, color->T by 1 along the path. The path is S -> i0 -> a -> ... -> b -> T. The item-color edges along the path:
- S->i0 and i0->a: item i0 becomes assigned to a (flow on i0->a +1).
- For transition a->b direct: residual path a->i1 (reverse of i1->a) means flow on i1->a decreases by 1; then i1->b increases by 1. So i1 moves from a to b.
- For transition via S: a->i1 (decrease i1->a), i1->S (decrease S->i1? Wait S->i1 is an edge with flow 1 if i1 used. The residual path uses i1->S, which is reverse of S->i1, so flow on S->i1 decreases by 1, meaning item i1 becomes unused. Then S->j (increase S->j) and j->b (increase j->b), so j becomes used and assigned to b.)
- Finally b->T: flow on color b->T increases by 1. The number of items assigned to b after augmentation: It gains from previous transition (either i1 or j) and loses? The flow on b->T doesn't correspond to an item assigned to b; it's the total count of items assigned to b. So net flow count for color b increases by 1 (because b->T flow increases by 1). But if the path enters b via a transition, that transition already assigned an item to b, so count b +1. Good.
Thus flow counts update as described.

We need know the actual path chosen to update assignments. Since only 3 colors, we can reconstruct the shortest path from S to T by storing predecessor information in the color graph.

Let's design the unconstrained min-cost flow algorithm in detail.

Data structures:
- N, K, arrays X,Y,Z as list of tuples p[item][color] (0,1,2).
- current_color[item] = -1 for unused, 0/1/2 for assigned.
- flow_count[color] = number of items assigned to color.
- capacity = 2*K.

Heaps:
- unused_max[color]: max-heap of (p_i[color], item) for items currently unused. Since Python heapq is min-heap, store (-p_i[color], item). Initially all items unused, push all for each color. O(N) memory 3N.
- assigned_min_val[color]: min-heap of (p_i[color], item) for items currently assigned to color. We need min p_i^a among assigned to a. Push when item assigned to color a. Lazy check current_color[item] == a.
- assigned_diff[color a][color b]: min-heap of (p_i[a] - p_i[b], item) for items currently assigned to a. Need for each ordered pair a!=b. Push when item assigned to a. Lazy check current_color[item] == a. There are 6 heaps.

Additionally, for direct transition w[a][b], we need min over assigned to a of (p_i[a]-p_i[b]). This is assigned_diff[a][b]. For via S transition, need min_assigned_val[a] - max_unused[b]. max_unused[b] can be obtained from unused_max[b] (top after cleaning). min_assigned_val[a] from assigned_min_val[a].

But wait, the via S transition uses an item i assigned to a and an unused item j. The cost is p_i[a] - p_j[b]. To minimize, we choose min p_i[a] among assigned to a and max p_j[b] among unused. These are independent, so yes.

However, is it possible that the item i chosen for min_assigned_val[a] is the same as the item j chosen for max_unused[b]? No, i assigned, j unused. Distinct.

Now, when we augment along a path that uses a via S transition, we need retrieve the actual items:
- For direct transition a->b: pop from assigned_diff[a][b] to get item i (after cleaning). This item will move from a to b.
- For via S transition a->b: we need item i = min_assigned_val[a] (from assigned_min_val[a]) and item j = max_unused[b] (from unused_max[b]). The cost is p_i[a] - p_j[b]. We should use these items. But note, if the path uses multiple transitions, the same item i might be selected for min_assigned_val[a] and also for a direct transition? We need ensure we don't use the same item twice in the path. Since the path is simple in the residual graph, we must avoid using the same item in two transitions. Could the shortest path algorithm choose edge a->b using item i, and later edge b->c using item i? As argued, item i is assigned to a, not b, so it won't be in assigned_min_val[b] or assigned_diff[b][c]. So no conflict. Could it choose item i for via S transition a->b, and also the unused item j for another via S transition later? j is unused, so it won't be in assigned_min_val for any color. It could be chosen as the unused item in another via S transition from some color to b? If the path goes ... -> b -> ... and later uses via S to b again, it might pick the same unused item j because j is still unused in the current residual graph (before augmentation). But a simple path cannot repeat vertices; if j was used in a previous transition, it would be a vertex in the path. The compressed color graph doesn't track unused item identities, so it might choose the same unused item twice in different via S transitions. Is that possible in a shortest path in the actual residual graph? Let's examine.

Suppose path: color a -> i1 -> S -> j (unused) -> color b -> ... -> color c -> i2 -> S -> j (same unused) -> color d. This would repeat vertex j, so not a simple path. In min-cost flow augmenting paths can be assumed simple (no repeated vertices) because cycles can be removed without increasing cost? Since residual costs can be negative? The unconstrained flow is optimal, so residual graph has no negative cycles. Thus shortest paths are simple. The compressed color graph with edge weights might produce a path that uses the same unused item in two via S transitions, which would not correspond to a simple residual path. Could that yield a lower cost than any simple path? If the same unused item is used twice, the path would have a cycle involving S and that item. Removing the cycle might change parity? Let's see. If a path uses the same unused item j twice, between the two uses, the path goes from j to some color ... back to S? Actually the first via S transition is a -> ... -> S -> j -> b. The second via S transition is c -> ... -> S -> j -> d. The subpath from j (after first) to j (before second) is j -> b -> ... -> c -> ... -> S -> j. This is a cycle starting and ending at j. Removing this cycle would yield a path from a to d that skips b,c and the second S->j? But the cycle may have negative cost? Since residual graph has no negative cycles (unconstrained optimal), the cycle cost ≥0. Thus using j twice is not cheaper than removing it. So the shortest path in the compressed graph with edge weights that might reuse an unused item will not be strictly better; we can ignore or it will be corrected by shortest path distances? However, our w[a][b] via S uses max_unused[b] independently for each edge. If the same unused item is the max for multiple colors, the color graph might combine edges and overestimate? Actually it would use the same j in both edges, but the cost of the second edge would be min_assigned_c - p_j[d]. If j is the max unused for d, using it twice might give a lower cost than using two different unused items. But the path with repeated j is not simple, and the cycle cost may be nonnegative, so the simple path using two different unused items should have cost ≤ repeated-j path? Let's test. Suppose j has very high p for both b and d, so using it in both transitions seems cheap. But after the first transition, j would be assigned to b in the augmented flow, but before augmentation it's unused. In the residual path, using j twice means the path goes S->j twice, which is not allowed because S->j has capacity 1 (one unit of flow can be sent on that edge). In a flow, an edge can carry multiple units? S->j capacity is 1 (item can be selected at most once). In the residual graph, S->j has capacity 1. A circulation/path cannot use the same residual edge more than once in a simple path; but in a flow, multiple units could use it if capacity >1, but capacity is 1. So the compressed graph must respect capacities. The shortest path in the residual graph cannot use S->j twice because the edge capacity is 1. Our color graph edge weights ignore capacities and might allow using the same unused item in multiple edges, which would violate capacity. This is a serious issue.

Similarly, a direct transition a->b uses an item assigned to a. Could the same assigned item be used in two direct transitions? No, because it's assigned to only one color, so it can only be used for transitions out of that color. But if the path goes a->b (item i moves to b), then later b->c, it cannot use i because i is not assigned to b before augmentation. So no issue. The unused item issue is the only one.

How to handle unused item capacities in the compressed graph? We need ensure that each unused item is used at most once in the augmenting path. Since the number of unused items is large, but a path can use at most one via S transition? Actually a simple path in the residual graph can use S at most once? Let's see: S is a node. A simple path cannot repeat S. The path from S to T starts at S, so it leaves S once. It cannot return to S later because that would repeat S. Wait, the augmenting path starts at S and ends at T. In the residual graph, the path is S -> ... -> T. It can pass through S again? A simple path cannot repeat vertices, so it cannot return to S. Therefore, in a single augmenting path, S appears only at the beginning. But our color graph transitions via S include paths that go from a color to S and then to an unused item to another color. That would mean the augmenting path goes ... -> color a -> item -> S -> item -> color b. This returns to S after leaving it at the start! That would repeat S, which is not allowed in a simple path. Wait, is the augmenting path in the residual network allowed to revisit S? In standard shortest augmenting path, we look for a simple path from S to T; revisiting S would create a cycle, which can be removed. So indeed, an augmenting path should not return to S. Therefore, transitions via S (color -> item -> S -> unused item -> color) are NOT valid in an augmenting path from S to T because they revisit S. But in the circulation correction, paths between colors in R' (excluding T) can pass through S? For the correction circulation, we are finding cycles that include T. A cycle T->a ... b->T could pass through S? The cycle would include S and T. Is that allowed? A circulation can include S and T; cycles can pass through S. In the residual graph, a cycle can include S. For the unconstrained augmenting path, we should not use S in the middle because the path starts at S. However, standard min-cost flow augmenting paths are simple and start at S; they cannot return to S. But what about augmenting paths that use reverse edges into S? In residual networks, augmenting paths can go through S? Usually, we look for paths from s to t in the residual graph; they may pass through s again, but such paths contain cycles and can be simplified. Since there are no negative cycles in an optimal flow, the shortest path will be simple and not repeat s. So we can ignore paths that return to S. Therefore, for the unconstrained SSP, the color graph should NOT include via S transitions? Wait, but a transition from color a to color b via S corresponds to a residual path a -> item (assigned) -> S -> unused item -> b. If the overall augmenting path starts at S, this would revisit S. Is there another way to use an unused item in an augmenting path without revisiting S? The path starts at S -> unused item i0 -> color a. It can then go color a -> item i1 -> color b (direct reassignment). It cannot go to S again. So unused items other than the starting item cannot be selected in the same augmentation. But in the residual graph, there is an edge S->unused item; to use it, you must be at S. Since you can't return to S, you can only select one unused item per augmentation. This matches the fact that each augmentation increases the number of used items by 1; it starts by selecting one unused item, and then reassigns already used items. It does not unselect and select another unused item, because that would keep the count the same? Actually an augmenting path that returns to S and selects another unused item would increase the flow by more than 1? Let's check: S -> i0 -> a -> i1 -> S -> j -> b -> T. This path starts at S, leaves, returns to S, leaves again. It would increase flow on S->i0 and S->j, i.e., select two unused items, and also unselect i1. Net increase in used items = +2 -1 = +1, so it still increases total flow by 1. Is such a path valid in the residual network? It is a path from S to T that revisits S. It contains a cycle from S to S: S -> i0 -> a -> i1 -> S. This cycle has some cost. If the cycle cost is negative, the flow is not optimal; if nonnegative, removing it yields a path S -> j -> b -> T with lower or equal cost. Thus the shortest augmenting path will not include such cycles. So we can ignore via S transitions in the unconstrained SSP. Good! For the unconstrained min-cost flow, we only need direct reassignments between colors, not via S. The starting unused item is the only unused item selected.

But wait, what about an augmenting path that starts with S -> unused item i0 -> color a, then direct transitions, and ends at T. This is the standard alternating path for assignment. It never uses S again. So w[a][b] for SSP should be just min_{i assigned to a} (p_i^a - p_i^b), not via S. The via S transitions are relevant only for the parity correction circulation, where cycles can include S. In the correction, we need shortest paths between colors in R' (excluding T) and they may pass through S. So we need two different distance computations:
- For unconstrained SSP: use direct reassignment costs only.
- For parity correction: use full residual graph distances including S.

Let's verify with the counterexample. Unconstrained optimal f was found by SSP with direct reassignments. Starting from no assignment, augment 4 items. The optimal assignment A X, B X, C Y, D Z has cost -200. Good.

Now, for parity correction, we computed d[X][Y]=1 via X->A (cost50) + A->Y (cost -49). This is a direct transition, no S. d[X][Z]=1 direct. d[Y][Z]=50 direct. The via S transitions were not needed. But in general, correction paths may use S. We need include them.

Let's first implement unconstrained SSP with direct transitions only.

Unconstrained SSP algorithm:

State:
- current_color[item] = -1 (unused) or 0/1/2.
- flow_count[color] = number assigned.
- capacity = 2K.

Heaps for assigned items:
- For each color a and each b≠a, heap_diff[a][b] of (p_i[a]-p_i[b], item) for items assigned to a.
- For each color a, heap_val[a] of (p_i[a], item) for items assigned to a (for maybe not needed for direct SSP? We need s[a] from unused, and diff heaps. We don't need min assigned val for direct SSP. But for correction later we need assigned_min_val. We'll maintain anyway.)
- For unused, heap_unused[color] of (-p_i[color], item) for unused items.

Initialization:
- Push all items to unused heaps for each color.
- assigned heaps empty.
- flow_count = [0,0,0].

Each augmentation (repeat 2K times):
1. Clean unused heaps: while heap_unused[c] top item's current_color != -1, pop.
2. Compute s[a] = -heap_unused[a][0][0] (max p among unused) for each color a. But wait, if no unused items? Since total flow < 2K ≤ N, there is at least one unused item. Good.
3. Compute direct transition costs w[a][b] for a≠b:
   Clean heap_diff[a][b] while top item current_color != a, pop.
   w[a][b] = heap_diff[a][b][0][0] if heap nonempty else INF.
   Note: If a has no assigned items, heap empty, w=INF.
4. Compute shortest path distances d in the 3-node graph with edge weights w (direct transitions). d[a][a]=0. Use Floyd or Dijkstra (3 nodes). Also store next/pred for reconstruction.
   We need the shortest path from any start color a to any end color b, with cost s[a] + d[a][b] + t[b], where t[b]=0 if flow_count[b] < capacity else INF.
   Since capacity 2K and total flow <2K, at least one color has capacity; but some may be full.
   Let best_cost = min_{a,b} s[a] + d[a][b] + t[b].
   The augmenting path is: S -> unused item (best for start a) -> color a -> ... -> color b -> T.
5. Augment:
   - Get start item i0 = the unused item that achieved s[a] (top of heap_unused[a] after cleaning). We need pop it from unused heaps? We'll update current_color[i0]=a, so lazy deletion will handle unused heaps. But we should remove it from unused by setting current_color; no need to pop immediately. However, if we later query unused heaps, cleaning will pop stale entries. But for the current augmentation, we need to know i0. We can get from heap_unused[a][0][1] after cleaning. We should not pop it now? If we set current_color[i0]=a, the heap entry becomes stale; future cleaning will pop. That's fine. But if the same item is used as i0 and also appears in some transition? It is unused initially, so not in assigned heaps. Good.
   - Update flow_count[a] += 1.
   - Assign i0 to a: current_color[i0]=a; push i0 into assigned heaps for color a (heap_val[a] and heap_diff[a][b] for all b≠a).
   - Reconstruct the path from a to b in the color graph. For each edge u->v along the path:
        * Get the item i for direct transition u->v: clean heap_diff[u][v] top, item i with current_color[i]==u. This item will move from u to v.
        * Remove i from assigned heaps of u? We can just set current_color[i]=v and push into assigned heaps of v. The old entries in u's heaps become stale and will be cleaned later. No need to remove explicitly.
        * Update flow_count[u] -= 1; flow_count[v] += 1.
        * Push i into assigned heaps for v (heap_val[v] and heap_diff[v][w] for all w≠v).
   - Note: If the path length is 0 (a==b), no transitions.
   - The ending color b's flow_count was already incremented by the last transition? Let's trace: Start flow_count[a] +=1. For each transition u->v, flow_count[u]-=1, flow_count[v]+=1. If path a->...->b, the final transition into b increments flow_count[b]. If path length 0 (a==b), flow_count[a] already incremented. So total flow increases by 1. Good.

Need ensure that when moving item i from u to v, if v is the same as u? No, edges distinct.

Reconstruction of shortest path:
Since only 3 nodes, we can compute pred. Let's implement a simple function:
Given start a and end b, we can find the path by dynamic programming:
dist = [INF]*3; dist[a]=0; pred = [-1]*3.
Relax edges w[u][v] for u,v in 0..2, u!=v, if dist[v] > dist[u]+w[u][v]: update. Since 3 nodes, we can just run Floyd to get all-pairs and also pred? For reconstruction, we can compute the shortest path from a to b by trying all possible intermediate sequences (there are at most 6 simple paths). Simpler: after computing all-pairs d, to reconstruct path from a to b:
path = [a]
cur = a
while cur != b:
   find next v != cur such that d[cur][b] == w[cur][v] + d[v][b] (with d[b][b]=0). This works if w is direct edge cost and d all-pairs. Since 3 nodes, we can just loop v.
But note if d[cur][b] == d[cur][b] with no edge? If cur==b, stop. If there is no next, shouldn't happen.
We also need to know if the shortest path uses direct edges only (yes for SSP).

Now, after 2K augmentations, we have an unconstrained optimal flow f and cost C0. We can accumulate cost as we augment:
Each augmentation cost = best_cost (the min s[a]+d[a][b]+t[b]). C0 += best_cost.
At the end, C0 is the min cost (negative profit). We can also compute profit = -C0.

But wait, is the SSP with direct transitions and starting unused item sufficient to find the unconstrained optimum? Yes, this is the standard successive shortest augmenting path for the assignment problem with 3 colors. The residual graph's shortest augmenting paths from S to T can indeed be represented as S -> unused item -> color -> ... -> color -> T, with direct transitions between colors. They do not need to go through S because that would be a cycle. So correct.

Let's test SSP on counterexample manually:
N=4,K=2, capacity=4.
Initial unused all. s[a] = max p among unused:
X max 50 (A/B), Y max 50 (C), Z max 50 (D). s all -50.
No assigned, w=INF. t all 0. best_cost = min s[a] = -50. Suppose tie choose X. Augment: assign A to X (or B). flow X=1. C0=-50.
Next: unused B,C,D. s X=-50 (B), Y=-50 (C), Z=-50 (D). assigned A to X. w[X][Y]=p_A^X-p_A^Y=50-49=1; w[X][Z]=50-0=50; other w INF. best: start Y? s=-50 + d[Y][?] INF; start X s=-50 + w[X][Y]=1 + t Y = -49; start X + w[X][Z]=50 ->0; start Z s=-50. Actually best is start Z s=-50, no transition -> assign D to Z, cost -50. Or start Y assign C to Y -50. Depending tie. Suppose assign B to X (cost -50), flow X=2. C0=-100.
Next: unused C,D. assigned A,B to X. w[X][Y]=min(A 1, B 99)=1; w[X][Z]=min(A 50, B 1)=1. s X=-0? max X unused C=0,D=0 =>0; s Y=-50 (C); s Z=-50 (D). best: start Y s=-50 assign C to Y cost -50; start Z -50; start X s=0 + w[X][Y]=1 =1. Choose Y assign C to Y. flow Y=1. C0=-150.
Next: unused D. assigned A,B X, C Y. s X=0, Y=0? unused D Y=0, Z=-50. w[X][Y]=1, w[X][Z]=1, w[Y][X]=p_C^Y-p_C^X=50-0=50, w[Y][Z]=50-0=50. best: start Z s=-50 assign D to Z cost -50. flow Z=1. C0=-200. Unconstrained optimal found. Good.

Now, after unconstrained flow, we need compute parity correction.

We have the final assignment from SSP. We need compute the residual graph shortest path distances d_full[u][v] between colors in R' (excluding T but including S). We need include via S transitions. We can compute the 3x3 matrix of shortest path distances in the full residual graph R'. As argued, any path between colors can be represented as a path in a graph with 3 color nodes plus S? Actually R' has nodes S, items, colors. We can compute shortest paths between colors using a similar color graph but with edge weights that include both direct transitions and via S transitions. However, we must be careful about capacities of unused items and S in the correction. The correction circulation can use S multiple times? A circulation is a set of cycles; it can include S multiple times if it consists of multiple cycles, but the total flow on S->unused item edges has capacity 1 per item. The T-join paths we combine may each use S. We need ensure capacities are respected. The minimum cost circulation with parity O in the residual graph should respect capacities. Our T-join formulation using shortest paths between colors in R' assumes we can take paths independently, but they might share edges/items, violating capacities. Is that possible? We need examine.

The correction circulation δ = f' - f. It can use residual edges up to their capacities. The T-join decomposition into paths between colors may share items. For example, the optimal correction in the counterexample used two paths: X->Y and X->Z. They shared the reverse edge T->X? Wait each path as a cycle through T used T->X (reverse of color X->T) twice. The capacity of T->X is f_X = 2, so using it twice is okay. They used different items (A and B) for the transitions. They did not share item edges. In general, the T-join paths might share an item edge if the same item is used in two paths. Is that allowed? A residual edge has capacity 1 for item-color edges (since original item->color cap1). If two paths use the same item-color residual edge, the circulation would have δ=2 on that edge, exceeding capacity. The minimum cost T-join in the color graph with edge weights d might choose paths that share an item, leading to an infeasible circulation. We need ensure this doesn't happen or handle it.

Let's analyze the structure of residual item-color edges. For a given item i assigned to color a:
- Reverse edge a->i has capacity 1 (since flow on i->a is 1).
- Forward edges i->b for b≠a have capacity 1.
A path from color u to v using item i:
- If u=a and v=b≠a: uses a->i and i->b. This moves item i from a to b.
- If u=a and v? via S: uses a->i, i->S, S->j, j->v. This unselects i and selects j.
Could two different paths use the same item i in the same direction? For item i assigned to a, the edge a->i has capacity 1. If two paths both start with a->i, infeasible. The shortest path distances d[u][v] might choose the same item i for multiple pairs, e.g., d[a][b] and d[a][c] both use item i (assigned to a) because it has low p_i^a - p_i^b and p_i^a - p_i^c. Then a T-join using both paths would use a->i twice, infeasible. Is there a way to avoid this? The minimum cost circulation with parity O should account for capacities. Our simple T-join on colors with distances may underestimate the correction cost if it reuses items.

Let's test if this can happen. In the counterexample, d[X][Y] used item A, d[X][Z] used item B, distinct. But could there be a case where the same item is the best for two transitions? Suppose item i assigned to X has very high X and low Y and low Z, so it is best to move it to Y and also to Z. A correction needing to toggle Y and Z might use paths X->Y and X->Z both using i, infeasible. The actual optimal correction might need to use a different item for one path, with higher cost. Our T-join with d would underestimate.

We need a more accurate way to compute the minimum cost circulation with parity O in the residual graph, respecting capacities. Actually an Eulerian subgraph has all vertices even degree, so it cannot have odd degree terminals. The parity of a specific edge being in S is a constraint on the edge, not on vertex degree. This is a "cycle space" constraint: we want a cycle (Eulerian subgraph) that contains a specified set of edges with odd multiplicity. In an undirected graph, the minimum weight Eulerian subgraph containing a specified set of edges with odd parity is the minimum weight T-join where T is the set of odd-degree vertices of the specified edge set? This is getting detailed, but since only 3 colors and at most 2 cycles, we can enumerate all possible ways to combine directed paths between colors to achieve parity O, and check T arc capacities. The cost is the sum of the directed path costs. The paths themselves are in R' (excluding T) and can be chosen as shortest directed paths. They may share item edges; if they do, the symmetric difference might yield a cheaper feasible circulation, but the enumeration of directed paths with shortest distances might overestimate. However, the true minimum circulation can be decomposed into cycles through T. Each cycle through T corresponds to a directed path between two colors in R'. The parity of the set of cycles is the symmetric difference of their endpoint pairs. To achieve O={a,b}, we can use:
- One cycle with path a->b or b->a.
- Two cycles with paths that together toggle a,b. The possible endpoint pairs for two paths:
   * Paths a->c and b->c (both end at c): toggles a,c and b,c -> a,b.
   * Paths c->a and c->b (both start at c): toggles a,b.
   * Paths a->c and c->b: toggles a,c and c,b -> a,b.
   * Paths c->a and b->c: toggles a,b.
In general, any two paths whose endpoints (as unordered pairs) have odd degrees at a,b. The cost is sum of the shortest directed path costs for the chosen ordered paths. If the two paths share residual edges, the actual circulation cost would be less than the sum (because shared edges flow 2? Actually if two cycles share an edge, the flow on that edge is 2, which is allowed if capacity ≥2. The cost is 2*cost(edge), so sum of cycle costs counts it twice, which is correct. If capacity is 1, sharing is not allowed. But if they share an edge with capacity 1, the sum of cycle costs is not feasible. However, we can take the symmetric difference of the two cycles to get a circulation with the same parity and cost ≤ sum, and it will not share that edge. The symmetric difference of two cycles is a circulation (mod2) but may not be a directed circulation? The symmetric difference of two directed cycles is an Eulerian undirected graph; it can be oriented as a directed circulation? The actual integer circulation δ = cycle1 + cycle2 has flow 2 on shared edges and 1 on others. If we instead use δ' = δ - 2*(shared edges) (i.e., cancel shared edges), the flow on shared edges becomes 0, and the cost decreases by 2*cost(shared). Is δ' still a circulation? Yes, subtracting twice a set of edges that forms a circulation? The shared edges between two cycles form a collection of paths; subtracting 2*shared may break flow conservation. But mod2, the symmetric difference is a circulation over GF(2); over integers, we need a nonnegative circulation. If the shared edges form a path from u to v, cycle1 uses it in one direction, cycle2 uses it in the same direction? If they share edges in the same direction, then cycle1 = P_shared + R1, cycle2 = P_shared + R2. The sum has 2*P_shared + R1 + R2. Subtracting 2*P_shared leaves R1+R2. Is R1+R2 a circulation? R1 is a path from v to u (complement of P_shared in cycle1), R2 is a path from v to u. Their sum is not necessarily a circulation (two paths from v to u). But the symmetric difference of cycles is a circulation mod2, not necessarily integer. To get an integer circulation, we could instead take the sum of R1 and the reverse of R2? Not available. So cancellation may not yield a feasible integer circulation. However, in min-cost flow, if two augmenting paths share edges, the combined flow is feasible if capacities allow flow 2 on shared edges. If capacity is 1, the paths cannot both be used. The shortest path distances d do not account for capacity conflicts. This is a real issue.

But maybe in the residual graph of an optimal flow, the shortest paths between colors are edge-disjoint for the combinations we need, or if not, the capacity conflict can be resolved by choosing the next shortest path. We need a robust algorithm.

How to compute the minimum correction exactly? This is a small min-cost flow problem on the residual graph with at most 2 units of parity. Let's formulate.

We want to find a minimum cost set of cycles in R whose parity is O. This is equivalent to finding a minimum cost flow of 2 units? Consider adding a dummy edge from T to a super source? Augmenting along a path in the color graph:
- The path is a sequence of colors c0 -> c1 -> ... -> cm.
- For each edge u->v in the path, we need perform a "move" from u to v using either direct or via S, whichever achieved w[u][v]. We need know which type and which items.
   * Direct: get item i from assigned_diff[u][v] (cleaned). Move i from u to v.
   * Via S: get item i from assigned_min_val[u] (cleaned) and item j from unused_max[v] (cleaned). Move i from u to unused, and j from unused to v. This is a "swap" of selected status.
- Additionally, if the source color is a source in the correction flow, does the path start at a color with an "extra" item? In the unconstrained SSP, the path started at S with an unused item, which increased flow by 1. In the correction min-cost flow, the path starts at a source color and ends at a sink color. This represents a cycle through T in the original residual graph. The augmentation along a path in R' does not change the total number of selected items; it just reassigns items and possibly swaps with unused. The flow value in the correction min-cost flow corresponds to the number of cycles, not items. Each path from source color to sink color, when combined with T arcs, forms a cycle. The augmentation in R' should update the assignment according to the path, but the total flow count remains the same? Let's check.

For a single path a->b (direct moves), moving items along the path changes color counts: source color a loses? Actually a path a->...->b in R' starts at color a and ends at color b. The residual path uses reverse edges out of a and forward edges into b. If we augment along this path in R' (as a flow from a to b), the net effect on color counts is: a loses 1 item, b gains 1 item. Intermediate colors unchanged. This matches the cycle T->a + path + b->T, which decreases f_a by 1 and increases f_b by 1. Total selected items unchanged. Good.

For a path that uses via S, e.g., a -> item i (assigned a) -> S -> unused j -> b. Net effect: item i becomes unused (a loses 1), item j becomes assigned to b (b gains 1). Total selected items unchanged. Good.

So augmenting along a path in R' changes the assignment but not total flow count. The flow_count array (number of items assigned to each color) should be updated: for each direct move u->v, flow_count[u]--, flow_count[v]++. For via S u->v, flow_count[u]--, flow_count[v]++ (same). The starting color loses? Wait, the path starts at color a. The first edge a->... uses an item assigned to a, so flow_count[a]--. The last edge ...->b assigns an item to b, so flow_count[b]++. Intermediate edges conserve. So overall, source color loses 1, sink color gains 1. This is exactly what we want for a cycle. In the unconstrained SSP, the path started at S with an unused item, so the first color gained 1; here the path starts at a color, so it loses 1.

Thus the augment function for correction is different from the unconstrained augment: it does not add a new item from S; it starts by removing an item from the source color.

We can implement `augment_path(state, path)`:
- For each edge u->v in path:
   * If direct: move item i from u to v.
   * If via S: move item i from u to unused; move item j from unused to v.
- This automatically updates flow_count: for each edge, flow_count[u]--, flow_count[v]++.
But wait, if the path is u->v->w, the first edge moves an item from u to v, so flow_count[v]++ and flow_count[u]--. The second edge moves an item from v to w. It should use an item that was assigned to v before the augmentation, not the item just moved from u. Our data structures and path reconstruction should ensure that the edge v->w uses an item currently assigned to v in the state before augmentation. After the first edge, the state has changed (item moved to v), so if we update the state before processing the second edge, the heap for assigned to v would include the moved item, and the shortest path edge v->w might choose it, which would correspond to using the same item twice? But in the residual path, the second edge v->w must use an item that was assigned to v before the augmentation, not the one just moved. However, if we update the state sequentially, the moved item is now assigned to v, and it might be chosen for the next edge, causing an invalid path (reusing the item). To avoid this, we should process all edges based on the original state, not the updated state. But the path was computed using the original state's edge weights; the edge v->w's chosen item was determined from the original state's heap for assigned to v. If we update the state before retrieving the next edge's item, the heap might give a different item. We need retrieve all items for all edges before modifying the state, or ensure the path reconstruction stores the specific item for each edge from the original state.

In the unconstrained SSP, we processed edges sequentially and updated state, but the path edges were chosen based on the original state. Could the same issue occur? For a path a->b->c, the edge b->c should use an item assigned to b in the original state. When we processed a->b first, we moved an item from a to b, adding it to b's heaps. In our earlier unconstrained SSP plan, we said we would reconstruct the path and for each edge get the item from the heap after cleaning, then move it. However, in the unconstrained SSP, the path a->b->c uses two distinct items: one assigned to a, one assigned to b originally. The item moved from a to b is not originally assigned to b, so it should not be in the original heap for b. But after we move it, it is in the heap. If we query the heap for b after the move, it might be chosen if it has a better cost than the original item. This would be wrong. We can detect and avoid by, when querying, if the unused item is already reserved, we need the next best unused item. How to compute the correction exactly and efficiently? We want the minimum cost circulation with parity O. As argued, it can be one of the configurations. We can create a super source SS and super sink TT:
- Add SS -> a and SS -> b? Let's think. The correction can be seen as sending 2 units of "parity" from the odd colors to the even color or vice versa. Let's implement a function `min_cost_flow_Rprime(sources, sinks, demand)` that computes the minimum cost to send `demand` units from the multiset of source colors to the multiset of sink colors in the residual graph R', using the current unconstrained optimal assignment as the initial state. We can run the SSP on the full residual graph R' but with a super source and sink. Since demand ≤2, we can implement it by repeatedly finding the shortest augmenting path from any source with remaining supply to any sink with remaining demand in the current residual graph, updating a temporary state. The shortest augmenting path can be found using the compressed color graph with edge weights w[u][v] (including via S), but we must ensure the path is valid (no item reuse). For a single augmenting path, the compressed shortest path might reuse an item within the path (e.g., path X->Y->Z using the same item for X->Y and Y->Z? Impossible because item assigned to X not Y; but via S could reuse unused item). For a path of length ≤2, we can validate and if invalid, find the next best path. However, since we run SSP and update the state after each augmentation, the second augmentation's shortest path will automatically avoid items used in the first augmentation because the state has changed. The issue is only within a single augmenting path reusing an item. A single augmenting path in the residual graph is simple; the compressed path might reuse an unused item in two via S edges. Can a shortest path of length ≤2 reuse an unused item? Example path X->Y via S uses unused item j, then Y->Z via S uses the same unused item j. Is that possible in the compressed graph? The edge X->Y via S cost = min_assigned_X - max_unused_Y. The edge Y->Z via S cost = min_assigned_Y - max_unused_Z. They could both use the same unused item j if j has the max value for both Y and Z. The compressed path would be invalid. To avoid this, when computing the shortest path in the color graph, we need to know if the via S items conflict.
