Maybe we can avoid via S in the correction by noting that the unconstrained optimal flow already selected the best items; swapping with unused items might be needed but can be represented as a path that goes through S. We can handle via S by treating S as a color? Let's include S as a node in the color graph? The residual graph R' has S connected to unused items. A via S transition u -> S -> v uses an item assigned to u and an unused item. This is like going from u to S (cost min_assigned_u) and S to v (cost -max_unused_v). If we include S as a node, then a path u -> S -> v is just two edges. The shortest path in the full graph can be computed with nodes {0,1,2,S}. Since S is a single node, we can run Dijkstra/Bellman-Ford on a 4-node graph with edge weights:
- u -> S: min_{i assigned to u} p_i^u (cost to unselect an item from u)
- S -> v: min_{j unused} (-p_j^v) = -max_unused_v (cost to select an unused item for v)
- u -> v direct: min_{i assigned to u} (p_i^u - p_i^v)
- S -> S? 0
- v -> S? also possible? From color v to S uses an item assigned to v, same as u->S.
- S -> u? from S to color u uses an unused item, same as S->v.
This 4-node graph captures all paths in R'! Because any path in R' alternates between colors and items, but the item-specific costs are summarized by these edge weights. A path from color a to color b can go through S multiple times, but a shortest simple path won't repeat S. With 4 nodes, we can compute shortest paths using Floyd-Warshall. This also handles via S transitions as paths a -> S -> b. The edge weights are minima over items, but could the same item be used for a->S and S->b? a->S uses an assigned item, S->b uses an unused item, distinct. Could the same unused item be used for S->b and later S->c? If the path goes a -> S -> b -> S -> c, it repeats S, so not simple; we can ignore. A shortest path won't repeat S if no negative cycles. Since the unconstrained flow is optimal, no negative cycles in R', so no negative cycles in the 4-node graph? The 4-node graph is a relaxation that may have negative cycles even if full graph doesn't? Let's check. A cycle a -> S -> a has cost min_assigned_a - max_unused_a. This corresponds to swapping an assigned item with an unused item of the same color. Could be negative? If there is an unused item with higher value in color a than an assigned item, the unconstrained flow would not be optimal (it would swap). So this cost should be ≥0. Similarly, cycles through colors correspond to reassignments; no negative cycles. So Floyd is safe.

Now, the 4-node graph edge weights still ignore item conflicts across different edges in a path. For a path a -> b -> c, the edges a->b and b->c use items assigned to a and b respectively, distinct. For a path a -> S -> b -> c, a->S uses assigned item, S->b uses unused item, b->c uses assigned item to b; distinct. For a path a -> b -> S -> c, a->b uses assigned to a, b->S uses assigned to b, S->c uses unused; distinct. The only potential conflict is if a path uses S twice (not simple) or uses the same unused item in two S->color edges (would require repeating S). So a simple path in the 4-node graph corresponds to a valid path in R' with distinct items. Great! This solves the within-path conflict.

Thus we can compute shortest paths in R' between colors by running Floyd-Warshall on the 4-node graph (colors + S). Edge weights:
- For colors u,v (u≠v):
   direct[u][v] = min assigned to u of (p_i[u] - p_i[v]).
- For color u to S:
   toS[u] = min assigned to u of p_i[u].
- For S to color v:
   fromS[v] = - max unused p_i[v] (i.e., min unused of -p_i[v]).
- S to S = 0.
- Color u to color v also can go via S, but Floyd will handle that if we include edges u->S and S->v. We don't need a direct via S edge.
- Color u to color v direct edge as above.
- What about color u to color v via an item assigned to u and then directly to v (without S)? That's direct edge.
- What about color u to S to color v: uses assigned item and unused item, handled by u->S + S->v.
- What about color u to color v via another color w: handled by Floyd.

This 4-node graph is excellent. We can compute all-pairs shortest distances between colors in R' using Floyd on 4 nodes. This gives d[u][v] for colors, including paths that go through S and other colors. It automatically uses the best items for each edge, and since a simple path in the 4-node graph has no repeated S/colors, item conflicts across edges are avoided (items for different edges are from different color classes or unused vs assigned). Need ensure that a path color u -> S -> color v uses an assigned item from u and an unused item for v. If the same unused item is the best for S->v and also for S->w in a path u->S->v->w? The path u->S->v->w does not use S again, so no conflict. Good.

Now, for the correction min-cost flow with up to 2 units, we can use the 4-node graph to find the shortest augmenting path in the current temporary state. After augmenting along a path, the state changes (items move), and the edge weights in the 4-node graph change. We can update the state and recompute edge weights for the next augmentation. Since demand ≤2, this is easy.

We need implement the augmentation along a path in the 4-node graph. The path may include S as an intermediate node. We need know for each edge:
- If edge is color u -> color v direct: move item from u to v (the one that achieved direct[u][v]).
- If edge is color u -> S: unselect an item from u (the one with min p_i[u]).
- If edge is S -> color v: select an unused item for v (the one with max p_i[v]).
- If edge is S -> S: no-op.
A path from source color to sink color in the 4-node graph will start at a color and end at a color, possibly with S in between. We can reconstruct the sequence of nodes. Then apply the moves:
For each consecutive pair (u,v):
- If u is color and v is color: direct move item from u to v.
- If u is color and v is S: move item from u to unused (unselect).
- If u is S and v is color: move item from unused to v (select).
- If u is S and v is S: none.
This will update flow_count: color u to color v: u--, v++. color u to S: u--. S to color v: v++. S to S: none. Overall, source color loses 1, sink color gains 1, as desired.

Now, the correction min-cost flow configurations:
We need compute the minimum cost to send:
1. 1 unit from a to b (or b to a). This is just the shortest path distance d[a][b] or d[b][a] in the 4-node graph. No need to run SSP; a single path has no conflicts with itself (4-node graph simple path). So cost1 = min(d[a][b], d[b][a]).
2. 2 units from even color c to odd colors a and b (one to each). This is a min-cost flow of 2 units in R': source c with supply 2, sinks a and b with demand 1 each. We can compute by running SSP for 2 augmentations on a temporary state. The first augmentation sends 1 unit from c to either a or b (whichever is cheaper). After updating the state, the second augmentation sends 1 unit from c to the other sink. The total cost is the sum. Since the state changes, item conflicts are handled.
3. 2 units from a and b to c: source a and b supply 1 each, sink c demand 2. Similarly.

We can compute configurations 2 and 3 by a generic `min_cost_flow(sources, sinks, demand)` that runs up to demand augmentations on a temporary copy of the state. Since demand ≤2, we can implement it by:
- Create a temporary state (copy current_color, flow_count, heaps).
- For each augmentation:
   * Compute 4-node graph edge weights from the temporary state.
   * Find the shortest path from any source color with remaining supply to any sink color with remaining demand. Since sources/sinks are colors, we can just compute all-pairs shortest paths in the 4-node graph and take min over source/sink pairs.
   * Augment along that path in the temporary state.
   * Add cost.
- Return total cost.

We need copy the state. Copying heaps as lists is okay. But we also need to update heaps when moving items. We'll implement a `State` class with methods:
- `clean_unused(color)`
- `clean_assigned_val(color)`
- `clean_diff(u,v)`
- `get_direct(u,v)`: returns (cost, item) after cleaning.
- `get_toS(u)`: returns (cost, item) after cleaning assigned_min_val[u].
- `get_fromS(v)`: returns (cost, item) after cleaning unused_max[v].
- `move_item(item, from_color, to_color)`: update current_color, flow_count, push to new heaps.
- `unselect_item(item, from_color)`: set current_color=-1, flow_count[from]--, push to unused heaps? Actually when an item becomes unused, we need add it to unused_max heaps for all colors. We can push (-p_i[c], item) for each c. It will be cleaned later.
- `select_item(item, to_color)`: set current_color=to_color, flow_count[to]++, push to assigned heaps.

When copying, we can just copy the current_color list and flow_count list, and copy each heap list. Since heaps contain tuples, copying the list is enough; the tuples are immutable. The heap property is preserved. We don't need to clean stale entries beyond what the methods do.

However, copying all heaps for each configuration might be heavy but acceptable. We need also copy the original p arrays? No, they are read-only.

Let's estimate: 12 heaps, each up to N=1e5, copying 3 times -> 3.6e6 tuple references, fine. But the heaps contain stale entries; after copying, the stale entries remain. The cleaning methods will pop from the copied heap, not affecting the original. Good.

We also need the original unconstrained optimal state after running SSP. We'll have the final `current_color`, `flow_count`, and heaps. We'll use that as the base for correction.

Now, let's refine the unconstrained SSP implementation to use the 4-node graph? For unconstrained SSP, the augmenting paths start at S (source) and end at T (sink). We can also use a 4-node graph? The unconstrained augmenting path is S -> unused item -> color -> ... -> color -> T. This is equivalent to a path from S to T in the full residual graph. In the color graph, we can model:
- S to color v: cost = -max_unused_v (select unused item for v).
- color u to color v direct: cost = min assigned to u of (p_i[u]-p_i[v]).
- color u to S? Not needed because path cannot return to S (would be a cycle). But for correctness, we can ignore.
- color u to T: cost 0 if capacity.
Then shortest path from S to T in this 3-color graph (plus S/T) gives the augmenting path. This is what we planned. We can implement unconstrained SSP with direct transitions only, no via S. We'll keep it as is.

After unconstrained SSP, we have the optimal assignment. Now correction configurations.

Let's test the correction algorithm on the conflict example:
Unconstrained assignment: A X, B X, C Y, D Z. flow X=2,Y=1,Z=1. Odd Y,Z, even X.
Compute d in 4-node graph:
- direct X->Y: min(A 100, B 1) =1 (item B)
- direct X->Z: min(A 100, B 1) =1 (item B)
- direct Y->Z: C Y-Z =100
- direct Z->Y: D Z-Y =100
- toS[X] = min p_X assigned = min(A100,B100)=100 (item A or B)
- toS[Y] =100 (C)
- toS[Z] =100 (D)
- fromS[Y] = -max unused Y. No unused items? N=4, K=2, all items used, so unused_max empty -> INF. fromS[Z]=INF. fromS[X]=INF.
Thus paths via S are INF. d[Y][Z] = min(direct Y->Z 100, Y->X + X->Z = d[Y][X]? d[Y][X] direct C Y-X=100, plus d[X][Z]=1 ->101, etc.) =100. So cost1=100.
Config 2: send 2 from X to Y,Z. First augmentation from X to Y cost d[X][Y]=1 using item B. After moving B X->Y, state: A X, B Y, C Y, D Z. flow X=1,Y=2,Z=1. Now need send from X to Z. d[X][Z] now: assigned to X only A, cost A X-Z=100. So total cost 101. Config 3: send from Y,Z to X. First Y->X cost C Y-X=100, state X=3? Wait moving C Y->X: flow X=3,Y=0,Z=1. Then Z->X cost D Z-X=100 total200. Min correction = min(100,101,200)=100. Correct!

Counterexample:
Unconstrained A X,B X,C Y,D Z. Odd Y,Z, even X.
d[X][Y]=1 (A), d[X][Z]=1 (B), d[Y][Z]=50, etc.

Now we need implement the temporary state and min-cost flow for configurations 2 and 3. Since demand ≤2, we can write a function `correction_flow(sources, sinks, demand)` that takes a list of source colors with supply (maybe duplicates) and a list of sink colors with demand. For config2: sources = [c, c] (supply 2 from c), sinks = [a, b]. For config3: sources = [a, b], sinks = [c, c]. It returns the min cost to send one unit from each source to each sink, with arbitrary matching, in the residual graph R'. Since sources/sinks are colors, and demand ≤2, we can run:
```
def min_cost_flow_small(base_state, sources, sinks):
    state = base_state.copy()
    total = 0
    remaining_sources = sources[:]  # each element is a color with 1 supply
    remaining_sinks = sinks[:]
    while remaining_sources:
        # compute all-pairs shortest distances in 4-node graph for state
        dist, next_node = floyd(state)
        best = INF
        best_si = best_ti = -1
        for si, s in enumerate(remaining_sources):
            for ti, t in enumerate(remaining_sinks):
                if dist[s][t] < best:
                    best = dist[s][t]; best_si=si; best_ti=ti
        if best >= INF/2: return INF
        total += best
        # reconstruct path from s to t in 4-node graph
        path = reconstruct(next_node, s, t)
        state.augment_path(path)
        # remove used source and sink
        remaining_sources.pop(best_si)
        remaining_sinks.pop(best_ti)
    return total
```
But note: If sources = [c,c] and sinks=[a,b], after first augmentation from c to a, we remove one c and a. The remaining source c and sink b. Good. The matching is chosen optimally by the shortest path at each step. Since the cost function is min-cost flow with 2 units, the greedy SSP is optimal for min-cost flow (successive shortest augmenting path) if all costs are nonnegative? Residual costs can be negative, but the initial state is optimal for the unconstrained problem, so no negative cycles. The SSP is correct for min-cost flow even with negative edges if potentials are used, but with only 2 units and no negative cycles, the greedy shortest augmenting path from current residual graph is correct for the min-cost flow of that value? In general, successive shortest augmenting path is correct for min-cost flow if we start from an optimal flow of value 0? Here we are starting from the unconstrained optimal assignment, which is not a zero flow in R'; but for the correction min-cost flow, the initial flow is 0 (we are sending additional flow in R'). The residual graph R' has no negative cycles because the base flow is optimal. The SSP algorithm for min-cost flow from zero flow in a graph with no negative cycles is correct: each augmentation along the shortest path yields the min-cost flow for the next value. So yes.

We need implement `floyd(state)` on the 4-node graph. Nodes: 0,1,2 for colors, 3 for S. Edge weights:
- dist[i][j] for i,j in 0..3.
Initialize INF, dist[i][i]=0.
For each color u:
  - toS[u] = state.get_toS_cost(u) (min p_i[u] assigned to u). If no assigned items, INF.
  - fromS[u] = state.get_fromS_cost(u) = -max p_i[u] unused. If no unused items, INF.
  - For each v != u: direct[u][v] = state.get_direct_cost(u,v). If no assigned to u, INF.
Set dist[u][3] = toS[u]; dist[3][u] = fromS[u]; dist[u][v] = direct[u][v].
Then run Floyd-Warshall on 4 nodes to get all-pairs shortest distances and next pointers.

But wait, the direct edge u->v uses an item assigned to u. If the path goes u -> S -> v, it uses an assigned item from u and an unused item for v. This is captured by dist[u][3] + dist[3][v]. Good.

What about a path u -> v -> S -> w? This is captured by direct u->v + v->S + S->w. The edge v->S uses an item assigned to v. Could the item used for direct u->v be the same as the item used for v->S? No, direct u->v moves an item from u to v; v->S uses an item assigned to v originally. Distinct. Good.

What about a path u -> S -> v -> w? S->v selects an unused item to v; v->w uses an item assigned to v originally. Distinct. Good.

Thus the 4-node graph is valid for simple paths.

- If u is color and v is S: return ('unselect', item) from assigned_min_val[u].
- If u is S and v is color: return ('select', item) from unused_max[v].
- If u is S and v is S: none.
Then in `augment_path`, we first collect all actions for each edge in the path. Since the path is simple and uses distinct item categories, the items should be distinct. But to be safe, we can check and if an item repeats, it would indicate a problem; however, the 4-node graph simple path should prevent repeats. We'll trust.

After collecting actions, apply them:
- direct: move item from u to v.
- unselect: move item from u to unused.
- select: move item from unused to v.

We need implement `move_item`:
```
def move_item(self, item, new_color):
    old = self.current_color[item]
    if old == new_color: return
    if old != -1:
        self.flow_count[old] -= 1
        # push to unused? No, moving directly to new_color, so it remains used.
    if new_color == -1:
        self.current_color[item] = -1
        # push to unused heaps
        for c in range(3):
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[c], (-p[item][c], item))
    else:
        self.current_color[item] = new_color
        self.flow_count[new_color] += 1
        # push to assigned heaps
        heapq.heappush(self.assigned_min_val[new_color], (p[item][new_color], item))
        for c in range(3):
            if c != new_color:
                heapq.heappush(self.assigned_diff[new_color][c], (p[item][new_color] - p[item][c], item))
```
But if old != -1 and new_color != -1, we decrement old and increment new. If old == -1 and new_color != -1 (select), increment new. If old != -1 and new_color == -1 (unselect), decrement old and push unused. We also need remove from old heaps? Lazy deletion handles it because current_color changes. When pushing to new heaps, old entries become stale.

For direct move, we call `move_item(item, v)` where old is u. For unselect, `move_item(item, -1)`. For select, `move_item(item, v)` where old is -1.

When copying the state, we need copy the heaps. We'll implement `copy`:
```
new = State(p, N, K)
new.current_color = self.current_color[:]
new.flow_count = self.flow_count[:]
new.unused_max = [list(h) for h in self.unused_max]
new.assigned_min_val = [list(h) for h in self.assigned_min_val]
new.assigned_diff = [[list(h) for h in row] for row in self.assigned_diff]
return new
```
Since the heaps are already heapified, copying a list preserves heap property.

- end cost to T: 0 if flow_count[u] < capacity else INF.
We can compute shortest path from a super source to super sink using Floyd on 3 colors? Since start costs and end costs are separate, we can just compute all-pairs shortest distances d_direct on 3 colors with direct edges, then best = min_{u,v} s[u] + d_direct[u][v] + t[v]. We don't need S in the middle. We'll implement a function `unconstrained_augment(state)` that returns cost and path.

Path reconstruction for unconstrained:
- Choose start color u and end color v that minimize.
- The path in color graph from u to v uses direct edges.
- The augmenting path is: S -> unused item (start_item) -> u -> ... -> v -> T.
- Apply:
   * select start_item to u (move from unused to u).
   * for each direct edge u->v in path, move item from u to v.
   * flow_count[u] increment via select, and direct moves adjust. The end v gets increment from last direct move. If path length 0 (u==v), only select start_item to u, flow_count[u]++.
This matches earlier.

We need retrieve start_item: from unused_max[u] after cleaning. Since we will select it, we should not pop it; just get top. Then call move_item(start_item, u). The stale unused entries will be cleaned later.

For direct edge u->v, retrieve item from assigned_diff[u][v] after cleaning. Then move_item(item, v).

Let's implement `floyd(state)`:
```
def compute_dist(state):
    INF = 10**30
    n=4
    dist = [[INF]*4 for _ in range(4)]
    nxt = [[-1]*4 for _ in range(4)]
    for i in range(4):
        dist[i][i]=0
        nxt[i][i]=i
    # direct color edges
    for u in range(3):
        for v in range(3):
            if u==v: continue
            c, item = state.get_direct(u,v)
            if c < INF:
                dist[u][v] = c
                nxt[u][v] = v
    # color to S
    for u in range(3):
        c, item = state.get_toS(u)
        if c < INF:
            dist[u][3] = c
            nxt[u][3] = 3
    # S to color
    for v in range(3):
        c, item = state.get_fromS(v)
        if c < INF:
            dist[3][v] = c
            nxt[3][v] = v
    # Floyd
    for k in range(4):
        for i in range(4):
            if dist[i][k] >= INF: continue
            for j in range(4):
                nd = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
                if nd < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = nd
                    nxt[i][j] = nxt[i][k]
    return dist, nxt
```
Note: `get_direct` cleans the heap and returns the cost and item. If heap empty, returns INF, -1. `get_toS` returns min assigned value and item. `get_fromS` returns -max unused and item.

But calling `get_direct` during Floyd cleans heaps, which modifies the state's heaps (popping stale entries). That's fine; it doesn't change the assignment. However, if we call `get_direct` multiple times, it may pop stale entries. Good.

For path reconstruction from dist/nxt:
```
def reconstruct_path(nxt, s, t):
    if dist[s][t] >= INF: return None
    path = [s]
    while s != t:
        s = nxt[s][t]
        path.append(s)
    return path
```
The path includes nodes 0..3 (3=S).

Now, `augment_path(state, path)`:
We need collect actions before modifying. For each consecutive pair (u,v) in path:
- if u == v: continue
- if u < 3 and v < 3: item = state.get_direct(u,v)[1]; action = ('move', item, v)
- if u < 3 and v == 3: item = state.get_toS(u)[1]; action = ('unselect', item)
- if u == 3 and v < 3: item = state.get_fromS(v)[1]; action = ('select', item, v)
- if u == 3 and v == 3: none
Collect actions. Then apply:
- move: state.move_item(item, v)
- unselect: state.move_item(item, -1)
- select: state.move_item(item, v)

But wait, if the path is color u -> S -> color v, the actions are unselect item from u, then select unused item for v. The order matters: if we unselect first, the unused set gains an item; then select might choose the just-unselected item if it is the max for v. Is that allowed? The residual path u -> item_i -> S -> item_j -> v uses item_i (assigned to u) and item_j (unused before augmentation). It cannot use item_i as item_j because item_i is not unused before augmentation. If we apply unselect first, item_i becomes unused, and then select might pick item_i, which would be invalid. To avoid this, we should select the unused item for S->v before unselecting, or ensure the selected item is not the one being unselected. Since we collected actions, we can apply select actions before unselect actions? But the path order is u->S then S->v; in the residual path, the unused item j is used after unselecting i, but j must be unused in the original state. If we apply select first, we need an unused item from the original state; the item i is still assigned, so not in unused heap. Then unselect i. This is valid. For a path with multiple S transitions? A simple path has at most one S. So we can apply all select actions (S->color) first, then unselect actions (color->S), then direct moves? Direct moves use assigned items from original state; if we unselect first, some assigned items become unused and might be chosen for direct? Direct heaps only contain assigned items; if we unselect an item, its direct entries become stale. But we already retrieved the direct item from the original heap; applying unselect before moving it could make it stale? We retrieved the item object; we can still move it. However, if a direct move uses an item that is also unselected in another action, that would be a conflict. A simple path won't have that. To be safe, we can apply actions in an order that doesn't affect retrieval:
- First, perform all direct moves? Direct moves change current_color of items, which could affect unselect/select if they involve the same item. But no conflicts.
- Since actions are independent, we can apply them in any order as long as we use the pre-retrieved items. The state's heaps will have stale entries, but we are directly setting current_color, not querying. The flow_count updates should be consistent. Let's just apply in path order; because we already retrieved the specific items, even if a heap would have chosen a different item later, we don't query again. The only issue is if an item is used in two actions; path simplicity prevents that. For S->v select, if we apply unselect first, the unused heap now contains the unselected item, but we already retrieved the select item from the original unused heap, so we won't accidentally select it. So path order is fine.

Now, `min_cost_flow_small`:
```
def min_cost_flow_small(base, sources, sinks):
    state = base.copy()
    sources = list(sources)
    sinks = list(sinks)
    total = 0
    while sources:
        dist, nxt = compute_dist(state)
        best = INF; bi=bj=-1
        for i,s in enumerate(sources):
            for j,t in enumerate(sinks):
                if dist[s][t] < best:
                    best = dist[s][t]; bi=i; bj=j
        if best >= INF//2: return INF
        total += best
        path = reconstruct_path(nxt, sources[bi], sinks[bj])
        augment_path(state, path)
        sources.pop(bi)
        sinks.pop(bj)
    return total
```
This computes the min cost for the given source/sink multiset.

Now, the unconstrained SSP also needs to update the base state. We'll implement `unconstrained_min_cost(state, K)`:
```
capacity = 2*K
total_cost = 0
for _ in range(2*K):
    # compute direct distances on 3 colors
    dist, nxt = compute_direct_dist(state)  # only direct edges, no S
    s = [state.get_fromS_cost(u) for u in range(3)]  # -max unused
    t = [0 if state.flow_count[u] < capacity else INF for u in range(3)]
    best = INF; best_u=best_v=-1
    for u in range(3):
        if s[u] >= INF: continue
        for v in range(3):
            if t[v] >= INF: continue
            cost = s[u] + dist[u][v] + t[v]
            if cost < best:
                best = cost; best_u=u; best_v=v
    total_cost += best
    # augment
    start_item = state.get_fromS_item(best_u)
    state.move_item(start_item, best_u)
    path = reconstruct_direct_path(nxt, best_u, best_v)
    for edge in path edges:
        item = state.get_direct_item(u,v)
        state.move_item(item, v)
return total_cost
```
We need `compute_direct_dist` that only uses direct color edges (no S). We can reuse compute_dist but ignore S? Simpler: write a function for 3-color direct distances.

Actually, the unconstrained augmenting path should not use S in the middle. We can compute direct distances on 3 colors with edge weights direct[u][v]. Since there are only 3 nodes, we can use Floyd. Let's implement `get_fromS_cost` and `get_fromS_item`: from unused_max[v], clean, return -heap[0][0] and item. If no unused, INF.

`get_toS_cost` and `get_toS_item`: from assigned_min_val[u], clean, return heap[0][0] and item. If empty, INF.

When moving an item to a new color, we push to heaps. For unused heaps, we push for all colors. For assigned heaps, push to assigned_min_val[new_color] and assigned_diff[new_color][c] for c != new_color.

Now, we need be careful with the unconstrained SSP optimality. The unconstrained problem is a min-cost flow with capacities on color->T = 2K. The SSP with direct transitions and selecting unused items should find the optimal assignment of 2K items to colors. Let's verify with a case where an augmenting path might need to go through S (unselect an item and select another) to improve cost. In the unconstrained problem, each augmentation increases the number of selected items by 1. It starts by selecting one unused item. It may also unselect a previously selected item and select another unused item if the path returns to S, but we argued such paths contain cycles and are not needed for optimality. Is that always true for min-cost flow? In standard assignment, augmenting paths alternate between unmatched and matched items and never revisit the source. They can include reverse edges from items to source? In the residual graph, the source S has edges to all unmatched items and matched items have edges to S. An augmenting path from S to T could go S -> unmatched item -> color -> matched item -> S -> unmatched item -> color -> T. This revisits S. Such a path would increase flow by 1 but also swap two unmatched items. The cycle S->...->S has some cost. If the cycle cost is negative, the current flow is not optimal; if nonnegative, removing it gives a cheaper or equal path. Since we maintain an optimal flow for the current value, there are no negative cycles, so the shortest augmenting path can be assumed not to revisit S. Thus direct transitions only are sufficient. Good.

What about a path that uses a reverse edge from a color to an item, then that item to S, then S to another unused item, then to a color, then T. This revisits S; not needed. So direct transitions suffice.

Now, after unconstrained SSP, the state is optimal for the unconstrained problem. We then compute correction. The correction min-cost flow uses the 4-node graph including S because cycles can revisit S (as part of a cycle, S can appear once). In a cycle T->a ... b->T, the path between a and b in R' can pass through S. This is allowed because the cycle includes S once, not a repeat (the cycle starts at T, goes to a, path to b, back to T; S appears at most once). Good.

Potential issue: The heaps for assigned_diff and assigned_min_val contain entries for items that may have been moved. Cleaning functions:
```
def clean_unused(self, c):
    h = self.unused_max[c]
    while h and self.current_color[h[0][1]] != -1:
        heapq.heappop(h)
```

    heapq.heappop(h)
```
Because an item assigned to u has entries in assigned_diff[u][v] for all v. If it moves to v, its entry in assigned_diff[u][v] becomes stale (current_color != u). Good.

When moving an item to a new color, we push to assigned_min_val[new] and assigned_diff[new][c] for c != new. We do not remove old entries; lazy.

When unselecting an item, we push to unused_max for all colors. The item's current_color becomes -1. Its old assigned entries become stale.

When selecting an unused item, we push to assigned heaps. The unused entries become stale.

- Select different unconstrained assignment? The even optimal 12 is assignment 2 Y,3 Y, not a small correction from 1Z,3Y? It changes selected items: unselect cake1, select cake2. This is a via S path: Z -> S -> Y? Let's see correction from 1Z,3Y to 2Y,3Y: unselect cake1 (Z), select cake2 (Y). This toggles Z and Y. Cost = p_1^Z - p_2^Y = 8 - 5 = 3. This is a path Z -> S -> Y in R': Z -> item1 (assigned Z) -> S -> unused item2 -> Y. Cost = toS[Z] (min assigned Z =8) + fromS[Y] (-max unused Y = -5) =3. Our 4-node graph should capture this! I forgot to include via S in cost1. Let's compute d[Z][Y] in 4-node graph:
- direct Z->Y: item1 Z-Y =8-3=5.
- via S: Z->S cost toS[Z]=8, S->Y cost fromS[Y]= -max unused Y. Unused items: cake2 Y=5, cake? cake? cake3 is assigned Y, cake1 assigned Z, so unused cake2 Y=5. fromS[Y] = -5. Total 3. So d[Z][Y]=3. Correction cost1 = min(d[Y][Z], d[Z][Y]) = min(4,3)=3. Answer 12. Great! The 4-node graph handles via S. Good.

So the correction algorithm works for sample 1.

Now, we need ensure the unconstrained SSP selects an optimal assignment. In sample 1, it might select 1Z and 3Y. Good.

Let's implement and finalize.

One more detail: In `compute_dist` for correction, the edge weights direct[u][v] use assigned_diff[u][v]. But if u has no assigned items, direct INF. toS[u] INF. fromS[v] uses unused_max; if no unused, INF. Floyd with INF large.

In `min_cost_flow_small`, after first augmentation, the state changes. We copy the base state. The base state's heaps contain stale entries; copying preserves them. The cleaning methods will pop stale entries from the copied heaps, not affecting base. Good.

Potential performance: Copying heaps for each correction config (at most 2 configs, since cost1 uses dist without copy) and running up to 2 augmentations each. Each augmentation computes Floyd on 4 nodes, which calls get_direct/get_toS/get_fromS, each cleaning heaps. Cleaning may pop many stale entries over time, but total O(N log N). Copying heaps O(N) per config. Overall O(N log N). Good.

Let's write the final code.

Need be careful with INF. Max cost: N*1e9 ~1e14. Use INF=10**30.

Implementation plan in code:

```
import sys, heapq

def solve_case(N,K, cakes):
    p = cakes  # list of (x,y,z)
    state = State(p,N)
    cap = 2*K
    total_cost = 0
    for _ in range(cap):
        # compute direct distances
        dist, nxt = direct_dist(state)
        s = [state.fromS_cost(c) for c in range(3)]
        t = [0 if state.flow_count[c] < cap else INF for c in range(3)]
        best = INF; bu=bv=-1
        for u in range(3):
            if s[u] >= INF: continue
            for v in range(3):
                if t[v] >= INF: continue
                cost = s[u] + dist[u][v] + t[v]
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost; bu=u; bv=v
        total_cost += best
        # augment
        start_item = state.fromS_item(bu)
        state.move_item(start_item, bu)
        path = reconstruct_direct(nxt, bu, bv)
        for i in range(len(path)-1):
            u=path[i]; v=path[i+1]
            item = state.direct_item(u,v)
            state.move_item(item, v)
    # correction
    odd = [c for c in range(3) if state.flow_count[c] % 2 == 1]
    correction = 0
    if odd:
        a,b = odd[0], odd[1]
        c = 3 - a - b  # since sum 3
        dist, nxt = compute_dist(state)
        cost1 = min(dist[a][b], dist[b][a])
        cost2 = min_cost_flow_small(state, [c,c], [a,b])
        cost3 = min_cost_flow_small(state, [a,b], [c,c])
        correction = min(cost1, cost2, cost3)
    ans = -total_cost - correction
    return ans
```

Wait, `total_cost` is the min cost (negative profit). The final profit = -total_cost - correction. Because correction is additional cost (positive) to fix parity. In sample 1, total_cost=-15, correction=3, ans=12. Good.

But is `correction` always positive? It should be. If odd empty, correction=0.

Let's write the State class:

```
class State:
    def __init__(self, p, N):
        self.p = p
        self.N = N
        self.current_color = [-1]*N
        self.flow_count = [0,0,0]
        self.unused_max = [[] for _ in range(3)]
        self.assigned_min = [[] for _ in range(3)]
        self.assigned_diff = [[[] for _ in range(3)] for _ in range(3)]
        for i in range(N):
            xi, yi, zi = p[i]
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[0], (-xi, i))
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[1], (-yi, i))
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[2], (-zi, i))
```

Methods:
```
def clean_unused(self, c):
    h = self.unused_max[c]
    while h and self.current_color[h[0][1]] != -1:
        heapq.heappop(h)
```

self.clean_unused(c)
if not self.unused_max[c]: return INF
return -self.unused_max[c][0][0]
```

```

return self.unused_max[c][0][1]
```

```

return self.assigned_min[c][0][0]
```
`toS_item(c)` similar.

    heapq.heappop(h)
```

if u==v: return 0
self.clean_diff(u,v)
if not self.assigned_diff[u][v]: return INF
return self.assigned_diff[u][v][0][0]
```
`direct_item(u,v)` similar.

`move_item(item, new_color)`:
```
old = self.current_color[item]
if old == new_color: return
if old != -1:
    self.flow_count[old] -= 1
if new_color == -1:
    self.current_color[item] = -1
    xi, yi, zi = self.p[item]
    heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[0], (-xi, item))
    heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[1], (-yi, item))
    heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[2], (-zi, item))
else:
    self.current_color[item] = new_color
    self.flow_count[new_color] += 1
    val = self.p[item][new_color]
    heapq.heappush(self.assigned_min[new_color], (val, item))
    for c in range(3):
        if c != new_color:
            heapq.heappush(self.assigned_diff[new_color][c], (val - self.p[item][c], item))
```
Note: If old != -1 and new_color != -1, we decrement old and increment new. If old == -1 and new_color != -1, increment. If old != -1 and new_color == -1, decrement. Good.

`copy()`:
```
new = State.__new__(State)
new.p = self.p
new.N = self.N
new.current_color = self.current_color[:]
new.flow_count = self.flow_count[:]
new.unused_max = [list(h) for h in self.unused_max]
new.assigned_min = [list(h) for h in self.assigned_min]
new.assigned_diff = [[list(h) for h in row] for row in self.assigned_diff]
return new
```
Using `__new__` avoids reinitializing and pushing all items.

Now, `direct_dist(state)`:
```
def direct_dist(state):
    INF = 10**30
    dist = [[INF]*3 for _ in range(3)]
    nxt = [[-1]*3 for _ in range(3)]
    for i in range(3):
        dist[i][i]=0; nxt[i][i]=i
    for u in range(3):
        for v in range(3):
            if u==v: continue
            c = state.direct_cost(u,v)
            if c < INF:
                dist[u][v]=c; nxt[u][v]=v
    for k in range(3):
        for i in range(3):
            for j in range(3):
                if dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k]+dist[k][j]
                    nxt[i][j] = nxt[i][k]
    return dist, nxt
```
`reconstruct_direct(nxt, s, t)`:
```
path=[s]
while s!=t:
    s=nxt[s][t]
    path.append(s)
return path
```

`compute_dist(state)` for 4 nodes:
Similar with nodes 0..2 colors, 3=S.
```
def compute_dist(state):
    INF=10**30
    n=4
    dist=[[INF]*n for _ in range(n)]
    nxt=[[-1]*n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        dist[i][i]=0; nxt[i][i]=i
    for u in range(3):
        for v in range(3):
            if u==v: continue
            c=state.direct_cost(u,v)
            if c<INF:
                dist[u][v]=c; nxt[u][v]=v
    for u in range(3):
        c=state.toS_cost(u)
        if c<INF:
            dist[u][3]=c; nxt[u][3]=3
        c=state.fromS_cost(u)
        if c<INF:
            dist[3][u]=c; nxt[3][u]=u
    for k in range(n):
        for i in range(n):
            if dist[i][k]>=INF: continue
            for j in range(n):
                nd=dist[i][k]+dist[k][j]
                if nd<dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j]=nd; nxt[i][j]=nxt[i][k]
    return dist, nxt
```

`augment_path(state, path)`:
```
def augment_path(state, path):
    actions=[]
    for i in range(len(path)-1):
        u=path[i]; v=path[i+1]
        if u==v: continue
        if u < 3 and v < 3:
            item = state.direct_item(u,v)
            actions.append(('move', item, v))
        elif u < 3 and v == 3:
            item = state.toS_item(u)
            actions.append(('unselect', item))
        elif u == 3 and v < 3:
            item = state.fromS_item(v)
            actions.append(('select', item, v))
        else:
            # S->S should not happen
            pass
    for act in actions:
        if act[0]=='move':
            state.move_item(act[1], act[2])
        elif act[0]=='unselect':
            state.move_item(act[1], -1)
        elif act[0]=='select':
            state.move_item(act[1], act[2])
```

`min_cost_flow_small(state, sources, sinks)`:
```
def min_cost_flow_small(base, sources, sinks):
    state = base.copy()
    sources = list(sources)
    sinks = list(sinks)
    total = 0
    INF = 10**30
    while sources:
        dist, nxt = compute_dist(state)
        best = INF; bi=bj=-1
        for i,s in enumerate(sources):
            for j,t in enumerate(sinks):
                if dist[s][t] < best:
                    best = dist[s][t]; bi=i; bj=j
        if best >= INF//2:
            return INF
        total += best
        path = []
        s = sources[bi]; t = sinks[bj]
        # reconstruct
        cur = s
        path.append(cur)
        while cur != t:
            cur = nxt[cur][t]
            path.append(cur)
        augment_path(state, path)
        sources.pop(bi)
        sinks.pop(bj)
    return total
```

Now, unconstrained augment path reconstruction:
In unconstrained, after selecting start_item to bu, we need move along direct path from bu to bv. We can use `reconstruct_direct(nxt, bu, bv)`. Then for each edge, get direct_item and move. Note: If bu==bv, path length 1, no direct moves. The start_item selection increments flow_count[bu]. Good.

One subtlety: In unconstrained augment, when we select start_item to bu, the item is added to assigned heaps. Then when processing a direct edge bu->v, `direct_item(bu,v)` might return the start_item if it is the best for that edge. Is that valid? The augmenting path is S -> start_item -> bu -> ... If the path has a direct edge bu->v, it should use an item that was assigned to bu before the augmentation, not the start_item, because the start_item is just being assigned to bu in this augmentation. In the residual path, after S->start_item->bu, to go bu->v, we need a reverse edge bu->item for an item that was already assigned to bu before the augmentation. The start_item was not assigned to bu before, so it cannot be used. Our sequential update would add start_item to bu's heaps, and then `direct_item(bu,v)` might pick it, which is invalid! This is the same issue we identified. We must retrieve all direct items for the path before selecting the start_item, or ensure the start_item is not used for direct edges.

In the unconstrained augment, the path is S -> start_item -> bu -> ... -> bv. The direct edges should use items assigned to the respective colors in the original state (before selecting start_item). Therefore, we should retrieve the direct items for all edges before moving the start_item. Let's fix the unconstrained augment:

```
# before any moves:
path = reconstruct_direct(nxt, bu, bv)
direct_edges = []
for i in range(len(path)-1):
    u=path[i]; v=path[i+1]
    item = state.direct_item(u,v)  # from original state
    direct_edges.append((item, v))
# now augment:
start_item = state.fromS_item(bu)
state.move_item(start_item, bu)
for item, v in direct_edges:
    state.move_item(item, v)
```
But wait, `direct_item(u,v)` cleans heaps and returns the best item from the original state. If we call it before moving start_item, it won't see start_item. Good. However, if the path has multiple edges, the direct items are retrieved from the original state. They are distinct. Good.

Similarly, in `augment_path` for correction, we already collect all actions before applying, so it's safe.

Also, in the correction `min_cost_flow_small`, after the first augmentation, the state is updated. The second augmentation's path is computed on the updated state, so it's fine.

Let's update unconstrained augment code accordingly.

Another subtlety: In unconstrained, `s[u] = state.fromS_cost(u)` returns -max unused. The start_item is the max unused for color u. If we retrieve direct items before moving start_item, the start_item remains unused, so it won't be in assigned heaps. Good.

Now, in `direct_dist`, the direct edge costs are computed from the current state. They don't include the start_item because it's unused. Good.

Let's test sample 1 with this fix: Unconstrained augment 1: select cake1 Z (start), no direct edges, flow Z=1. Augment 2: s[Y]=-7 (cake3), s[Z]=-? unused cake2 Z=0, cake3? cake3 assigned Y, so unused cake2. s[Y]=-7, s[Z]=0. Direct edges from Z to Y: assigned cake1 Z-Y=5. Best start Y no transition cost -7 -> assign cake3 Y. flow Y=1. total -15. Good.

Correction as before.

Now, we need ensure `fromS_cost` and `fromS_item` clean unused heaps. If there are no unused items, INF. In unconstrained, before all 2K items selected, there is at least one unused, so s finite.

Let's write the final code.

Potential issue with `c = 3 - a - b` when odd colors are e.g., 0 and 2, c=1. Since colors are 0,1,2, sum=3, yes.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import heapq

INF = 10**30


class State:
    def __init__(self, p, n):
        self.p = p
        self.n = n
        self.current_color = [-1] * n
        self.flow_count = [0, 0, 0]

        self.unused_max = [[] for _ in range(3)]
        self.assigned_min = [[] for _ in range(3)]
        self.assigned_diff = [[[] for _ in range(3)] for _ in range(3)]

        for i in range(n):
            x, y, z = p[i]
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[0], (-x, i))
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[1], (-y, i))
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[2], (-z, i))

    def copy(self):
        new = State.__new__(State)
        new.p = self.p
        new.n = self.n
        new.current_color = self.current_color[:]
        new.flow_count = self.flow_count[:]
        new.unused_max = [list(h) for h in self.unused_max]
        new.assigned_min = [list(h) for h in self.assigned_min]
        new.assigned_diff = [[list(h) for h in row] for row in self.assigned_diff]
        return new

    def clean_unused(self, c):
        h = self.unused_max[c]
        while h and self.current_color[h[0][1]] != -1:
            heapq.heappop(h)

    def clean_assigned(self, c):
        h = self.assigned_min[c]
        while h and self.current_color[h[0][1]] != c:
            heapq.heappop(h)

    def clean_diff(self, u, v):
        h = self.assigned_diff[u][v]
        while h and self.current_color[h[0][1]] != u:
            heapq.heappop(h)

    def fromS_cost(self, c):
        self.clean_unused(c)
        if not self.unused_max[c]:
            return INF
        return -self.unused_max[c][0][0]

    def fromS_item(self, c):
        self.clean_unused(c)
        return self.unused_max[c][0][1]

    def toS_cost(self, c):
        self.clean_assigned(c)
        if not self.assigned_min[c]:
            return INF
        return self.assigned_min[c][0][0]

    def toS_item(self, c):
        self.clean_assigned(c)
        return self.assigned_min[c][0][1]

    def direct_cost(self, u, v):
        if u == v:
            return 0
        self.clean_diff(u, v)
        if not self.assigned_diff[u][v]:
            return INF
        return self.assigned_diff[u][v][0][0]

    def direct_item(self, u, v):
        self.clean_diff(u, v)
        return self.assigned_diff[u][v][0][1]

    def move_item(self, item, new_color):
        old = self.current_color[item]
        if old == new_color:
            return

        if old != -1:
            self.flow_count[old] -= 1

        if new_color == -1:
            self.current_color[item] = -1
            x, y, z = self.p[item]
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[0], (-x, item))
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[1], (-y, item))
            heapq.heappush(self.unused_max[2], (-z, item))
        else:
            self.current_color[item] = new_color
            self.flow_count[new_color] += 1
            val = self.p[item][new_color]
            heapq.heappush(self.assigned_min[new_color], (val, item))
            for c in range(3):
                if c != new_color:
                    heapq.heappush(
                        self.assigned_diff[new_color][c],
                        (val - self.p[item][c], item),
                    )


def direct_dist(state):
    dist = [[INF] * 3 for _ in range(3)]
    nxt = [[-1] * 3 for _ in range(3)]
    for i in range(3):
        dist[i][i] = 0
        nxt[i][i] = i

    for u in range(3):
        for v in range(3):
            if u == v:
                continue
            c = state.direct_cost(u, v)
            if c < INF:
                dist[u][v] = c
                nxt[u][v] = v

    for k in range(3):
        for i in range(3):
            for j in range(3):
                nd = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
                if nd < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = nd
                    nxt[i][j] = nxt[i][k]

    return dist, nxt


def compute_dist(state):
    # nodes: 0,1,2 = colors, 3 = S
    n = 4
    dist = [[INF] * n for _ in range(n)]
    nxt = [[-1] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        dist[i][i] = 0
        nxt[i][i] = i

    for u in range(3):
        for v in range(3):
            if u == v:
                continue
            c = state.direct_cost(u, v)
            if c < INF:
                dist[u][v] = c
                nxt[u][v] = v

    for u in range(3):
        c = state.toS_cost(u)
        if c < INF:
            dist[u][3] = c
            nxt[u][3] = 3

        c = state.fromS_cost(u)
        if c < INF:
            dist[3][u] = c
            nxt[3][u] = u

    for k in range(n):
        for i in range(n):
            if dist[i][k] >= INF:
                continue
            for j in range(n):
                nd = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]
                if nd < dist[i][j]:
                    dist[i][j] = nd
                    nxt[i][j] = nxt[i][k]

    return dist, nxt


def reconstruct_path(nxt, s, t):
    path = [s]
    while s != t:
        s = nxt[s][t]
        path.append(s)
    return path


def augment_path(state, path):
    actions = []
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u = path[i]
        v = path[i + 1]
        if u == v:
            continue

        if u < 3 and v < 3:
            item = state.direct_item(u, v)
            actions.append(("move", item, v))
        elif u < 3 and v == 3:
            item = state.toS_item(u)
            actions.append(("unselect", item))
        elif u == 3 and v < 3:
            item = state.fromS_item(v)
            actions.append(("select", item, v))

    for act in actions:
        if act[0] == "move":
            state.move_item(act[1], act[2])
        elif act[0] == "unselect":
            state.move_item(act[1], -1)
        else:  # select
            state.move_item(act[1], act[2])


def min_cost_flow_small(base, sources, sinks):
    state = base.copy()
    sources = list(sources)
    sinks = list(sinks)
    total = 0

    while sources:
        dist, nxt = compute_dist(state)
        best = INF
        bi = bj = -1

        for i, s in enumerate(sources):
            for j, t in enumerate(sinks):
                if dist[s][t] < best:
                    best = dist[s][t]
                    bi = i
                    bj = j

        if best >= INF // 2:
            return INF

        total += best
        path = reconstruct_path(nxt, sources[bi], sinks[bj])
        augment_path(state, path)

        sources.pop(bi)
        sinks.pop(bj)

    return total


def solve_case(n, k, cakes):
    state = State(cakes, n)
    capacity = 2 * k
    total_cost = 0

    # Unconstrained min-cost flow: choose 2k items and assign colors freely.
    for _ in range(capacity):
        dist, nxt = direct_dist(state)

        s = [state.fromS_cost(c) for c in range(3)]
        t = [0 if state.flow_count[c] < capacity else INF for c in range(3)]

        best = INF
        bu = bv = -1
        for u in range(3):
            if s[u] >= INF:
                continue
            for v in range(3):
                if t[v] >= INF:
                    continue
                cost = s[u] + dist[u][v] + t[v]
                if cost < best:
                    best = cost
                    bu = u
                    bv = v

        total_cost += best

        # Retrieve direct-move items before changing the state.
        path = reconstruct_path(nxt, bu, bv)
        direct_edges = []
        for i in range(len(path) - 1):
            u = path[i]
            v = path[i + 1]
            item = state.direct_item(u, v)
            direct_edges.append((item, v))

        start_item = state.fromS_item(bu)
        state.move_item(start_item, bu)

        for item, v in direct_edges:
            state.move_item(item, v)

    # Parity correction.
    odd = [c for c in range(3) if state.flow_count[c] % 2 == 1]
    correction = 0

    if odd:
        a, b = odd[0], odd[1]
        c = 3 - a - b

        dist, _ = compute_dist(state)
        cost1 = min(dist[a][b], dist[b][a])

        cost2 = min_cost_flow_small(state, [c, c], [a, b])
        cost3 = min_cost_flow_small(state, [a, b], [c, c])

        correction = min(cost1, cost2, cost3)

    return -total_cost - correction


def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    T = int(input())
    ans = []

    for _ in range(T):
        n, k = map(int, input().split())
        cakes = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
            cakes.append((x, y, z))
        ans.append(str(solve_case(n, k, cakes)))

    print("\n".join(ans))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
